# The Rise of Fascism: Italy, Germany and Japan - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches how three parliamentary or semi-parliamentary states of the 1920s stopped being governed by their parliaments, and it teaches that they stopped in three different ways, for reasons that only partly overlap. It begins with the word itself, because "fascism" is the one term in the world-history syllabus that candidates use most confidently and define least carefully: it teaches the features the first fascist movement actually claimed, the definitional tests that later scholarship built around those claims, and the reason the same core account that describes Italy and Germany as fascist states explicitly refuses that label for Japan, Spain and Portugal. It teaches the common interwar inheritance - disappointed victory, defeat disguised as betrayal, currency collapse, coalition immobilism, street violence, fear of revolution and then a world economic contraction - and it teaches why that inheritance produced authoritarian outcomes in some states and not in others. It teaches Italy first and completely: the "mutilated victory" and d'Annunzio at Fiume, the lira's collapse and the return of two and a half million ex-servicemen, the strikes and factory occupations of 1919 and 1920, the founding of the *fasci di combattimento* in 1919 with a socialist and republican programme, the electoral failure that produced the turn to property, the squad violence from late 1920, the thirty-five seats of May 1921, the failed general strike of summer 1922, the march on Rome of October 1922 that was a bluff which worked, and the king's decision not to resist. It teaches the construction of the regime step by step - the legalised militia, the Acerbo Law of November 1923, the election of April 1924, the murder of Giacomo Matteotti and the murder of Giovanni Amendola, the constitutional changes of 1925 and 1926, rule by decree, the reduction of the electorate, the abolition of elected mayors, the local *ras*, the twenty-two corporations, the Lateran Treaty of 1929, the Ministry of Popular Culture of 1937, the battles for the lira, for wheat and for births, land reclamation, *Dopolavoro*, and the racial legislation of 1938 that arrived after more than fifteen years of fascist rule. It teaches Germany as a different problem with a different answer: the republic's inherited stigma and the stab-in-the-back legend, proportional representation and eight-party Reichstags, the Spartacist rising and the Kapp Putsch, the assassinations, the Beer-Hall Putsch, the mark at twenty-one billion to the pound by November 1923, the Rentenmark, the Dawes loan and the prosperity that depended on it, the Wall Street Crash and the withdrawal of credit, unemployment past six million by the spring of 1932, Bruning's deflation, the Nazi seat curve that tracks the economy, the loss of thirty-four seats in November 1932, and the political intrigue of January 1933 that let Hitler into the Chancellery legally. It teaches what he did with it: the Reichstag fire and the emergency decree, the election of 5 March 1933 in which the Nazis still did not win a majority, the Enabling Law of 23 March, forcible coordination, the destruction of the unions, the one-party law, the Rohm purge of 30 June 1934, the death of Hindenburg and the oath, the youth organisations, the propaganda ministry, the churches, the police state and its recorded inefficiency, the Four-Year Plan, rearmament at fifty-two per cent of government spending in 1938-9, and the persecution of Jews from the boycott of April 1933 through the Nuremberg Laws of 1935 to Kristallnacht in November 1938. It teaches Japan as a third case with a different mechanism altogether: the Meiji settlement of 1868, the constitution of 1889 with an emperor who commanded the armed forces and a Diet that could initiate laws, the "Big Five" status of 1919, the wartime export boom and its collapse in 1921, the raw-silk catastrophe after 1929, corruption in the Diet, the elite hostility of the Peers and the Privy Council, the army's destruction of a conciliatory China policy in 1927, the occupation of Manchuria in September 1931 without the government's permission, the assassination of Prime Minister Inukai in May 1932, and the thirteen years in which the army more or less ran the country. It teaches the comparison properly - what the three regimes shared, where they differed, and why the differences decide the marks - and it teaches Spain and Portugal as the fourth and fifth cases that test the definition. It teaches mass mobilisation, propaganda, the leader cult, corporatist claims, organised violence, policy towards women and the young, and the bargains struck with business and with churches. It teaches consent and coercion together, because neither alone explains why these regimes lasted. Responsibility here is stated about governments, parties, ministries, militias, tribunals and named individuals, never about nations or peoples, and no mortality figure is asserted anywhere. The extermination of Europe's Jews, the wartime campaigns and the Pacific war belong to the Second World War owner; interwar diplomacy, the League and appeasement belong to the international-relations owner; the Russian Revolution and the Soviet state belong to theirs; the Depression's economic mechanism belongs to the world-economy owner. This session owns the regimes themselves - how they were built, what they claimed, whom they rested on, and how far they differed.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is deliberate and it is not the order of a textbook chapter. A chapter account gives Italy, then Germany, then Japan, and lets the reader infer the comparison. That produces the standard failing script: three biographies in sequence, followed by a paragraph asserting that all three were basically the same thing. Here the definitional problem comes first, because a comparison is worthless until you know what is being compared and what the comparison is not entitled to claim. The common crisis comes second, because every national story afterwards is a variation on it. Then Italy is taught in four lessons and completely, because Italy is the case where the mechanism is visible in slow motion. Germany follows in five, because Germany is the case where the same vocabulary produces a different regime. Japan follows in two, because Japan is the case that tests whether the vocabulary applies at all. Only then does the session compare, and only then does it argue about consent, structure and agency.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Two labels run through every lesson, and keeping them apart is the habit that separates a confident answer from an over-confident one.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two canonical owners held for this topic, the one core world-history book read for this session, the official question paper, or a verified item from the single checking pass recorded in the final ledger | Assert it. Dates, laws, institutions, named individuals and recorded scholarly positions can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |

Six disciplines are specific to this topic. Each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where a fluent answer can be wrong in ways an examiner notices immediately.

**The attribution discipline.** Responsibility is stated about governments, cabinets, ministries, parties, militias, courts, army units and named individuals - never about nations or peoples. This is not delicacy; it is accuracy, and it is also the single most examinable habit in the topic. ⚠️ Formulations such as "the Germans wanted war", "the Italians were natural authoritarians" or "the Japanese were militaristic" are treated here as errors of fact. ✅ The core account itself records that when a boycott of Jewish shops was declared for 1 April 1933 the expected mass support did not appear, that the general public seemed apathetic and that some people showed sympathy - which is precisely the kind of evidence a collective formulation destroys. ✅ Where a held source uses a collective formulation - and one of the historians quoted in the core account does, bitterly and explicitly - it is reported as that writer's wording and is not adopted here.

**The no-equivalence discipline.** Comparison in this topic means grading, not levelling. ✅ The core account lists five similarities between the Italian and German systems and then six differences, and it is emphatic that the Italian system was not as ruthless or as brutal, that Italian fascism was not particularly anti-Jewish or racist until 1938, and that Italy never came near self-sufficiency or full employment while Germany eliminated unemployment. ⚠️ A script that writes "Mussolini and Hitler were essentially the same" has not compared; it has abolished the comparison. The same rule runs in the other direction: nothing here is a defence of any of these regimes, and grading the differences is an analytical operation, not a moral one.

**The evidentiary discipline for numbers.** This session uses only quantities recorded in its permitted sources, always with the measure stated, and never totals or extrapolates. ✅ The principal figures are these: Italian ex-servicemen at 2.5 million, the lira at 5 to the dollar in 1914 and 28 in 1921 and revalued at 90 to the pound instead of 150 in 1926, the thirty-five fascist seats of May 1921 against 123 socialist seats, about 50,000 blackshirts converging on Rome, 404 seats against 107 in April 1924, the electorate cut from about ten million to three million, twenty-two corporations by 1934, wheat imports cut by seventy-five per cent by 1935, Italian unemployment at 1.1 million, the complete mark series from twenty to the dollar at the war's end to twenty-one billion to the pound in November 1923, the £50 million reparations instalment paid in August 1921, the 1928 Reichstag composition party by party, German unemployment approaching four million by mid-1931 and over six million by spring 1932, the Nazi seat series from thirty-two in March 1924 to 230 of 608 in July 1932 and 196 in November 1932, 288 of 647 on almost ninety per cent turnout in March 1933 with forty-four per cent of those voting and thirty-six short of the 324 needed, the Enabling Law passed 441 to 94, unemployment down to just over two million by the end of 1935 and negligible by 1939, the military budget at fifty-two per cent of government spending in 1938-9, over seventy per cent of German households owning a radio by 1939, forty-three Gestapo officials for a city of 650,000, the Manchurian population of thirty million, and Japanese raw silk below one-fifth of its 1923 price by 1932. ✅ Every other quantity used anywhere in this session - book and artwork counts, film-crew numbers, party-seat totals, medal thresholds, age bands and the sterilisation figure - is likewise taken directly from a permitted source with the measure the source states. ❌ No figure is invented, adjusted, totalled or extrapolated, and ❌ no mortality figure is asserted anywhere in this session for any group.

**The sensitive-material discipline.** Persecution is taught through named instruments, named institutions and named decision-makers, with dates. ⚠️ It is not taught through atrocity description, and it is never quantified here. ✅ The extermination of Europe's Jews is recorded by the core account and is owned by the Second World War topic; this session stops at the outbreak of war and hands over explicitly in Lesson 10. Quotations from the dictators are not reproduced, with one deliberate exception noted in Lesson 14 where the core account's own analysis of a slogan is the teaching point.

**The anti-inevitability discipline.** ⚠️ "Inevitable" is used in this session only where a named scholar's position is being reported. ✅ The evidence base holds the disagreement openly: the core account records the view that by autumn 1932 nothing could have saved the republic, and it records Ian Kershaw's contrary judgement that there was no inevitability about Hitler's accession to power and that a Hitler Chancellorship might have been avoided. ⚠️ Those are rival readings of one record, and a script that states either as settled has misreported the state of knowledge.

**The boundary discipline.** Four neighbouring topics own material this one constantly touches. Interwar diplomacy, the League, Abyssinia as an international crisis, the Spanish Civil War as an intervention problem and appeasement belong to the international-relations owner; this session uses the foreign policies of these regimes only as evidence about the regimes. The Russian Revolution and the Soviet state belong to their own owner; this session uses only the fear of revolution as a political fact in Italy, Germany and Japan. The military course of the Second World War and the Holocaust belong to the Second World War owner. The causes, transmission and instruments of the Great Depression belong to the world-economy owner; this session uses the Depression as a causal variable and does not re-derive it.

## Roadmap

| Lesson | Dependency-led question | Stage |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | What does the word "fascism" have to do, and what can it not be made to do? | Foundation |
| 2 | Why did parliamentary government lose its claim to govern after 1918? | Foundation |
| 3 | Italy 1919-22: how did a movement with no seats become the only alternative? | Core |
| 4 | October 1922: was power seized, or was it handed over? | Core |
| 5 | Italy 1922-30: how does a coalition prime minister become a dictator? | Core |
| 6 | Italy 1926-39: what did the regime actually deliver, and what did it only claim? | Core |
| 7 | Germany 1919-29: what was the republic carrying, and what was its recovery resting on? | Core |
| 8 | Germany 1929-32: how did an economic collapse become a political one? | Core |
| 9 | Germany 1933-34: how was a legal appointment converted into a dictatorship? | Core |
| 10 | Germany 1933-39: how was exclusion built into law, and where does this topic stop? | Core |
| 11 | Germany 1933-39: was the economic record a success, and what was it for? | Core |
| 12 | Japan: what in the constitutional design allowed the army to escape civilian control? | Core |
| 13 | Japan 1921-37: how did party government end, and is "fascism" the right word for what replaced it? | Core |
| 14 | What did these regimes do to society - and how far did the machinery actually reach? | Advanced |
| 15 | Consent or coercion? Who carried these regimes, and why did they not fall? | Advanced |
| 16 | Comparison, causation and counterfactuals: converting all of this into marks | Synthesis |

*The map is the argument in outline. Notice its asymmetry: the Italian and German branches begin with a movement and end with a party-state; the Japanese branch begins with an institution - the army - and ends with an existing constitution still formally intact. ⚠️ That asymmetry is not decoration. It is the reason the comparison has to be graded, and it is the reason the last two lessons are about weighting rather than about narrative.*

## Lesson 1 - What the word has to do: defining fascism, and the limits of the comparison

Progress: 1/16 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: why a definition is needed before any comparison; the six features the first fascist movement itself claimed; the later definitional tests and what each one adds; why the same account calls Italy and Germany fascist and refuses the label for Japan, Spain and Portugal; the difference between fascism, authoritarianism and military dictatorship; what this topic owns and where it stops

### Visual: one word, four jobs, and the test that separates them

```text
   "FASCISM" IS ASKED TO DO FOUR DIFFERENT JOBS IN AN ANSWER.
   ONLY THE FIRST TWO ARE SAFE.

   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | JOB 1: NAME A SPECIFIC MOVEMENT AND REGIME                     |
   | Mussolini's movement of 1919 and the Italian state after 1922  |
   | STATUS: exact. This is what the word originally denoted        |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | JOB 2: NAME A FAMILY WITH SHARED FEATURES                      |
   | Italy and Germany: mass party, leader cult, one-party state,   |
   | national rebirth, anti-communism, organised violence, autarky  |
   | STATUS: defensible, IF the differences are graded              |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | JOB 3: NAME ANY INTERWAR RIGHT-WING DICTATORSHIP               |
   | Japan, Spain, Portugal, Argentina, Brazil                      |
   | STATUS: REFUSED BY THE CORE ACCOUNT. These had many similar    |
   | features but "were not, strictly speaking, fascist states:     |
   | they lacked the vital element of mass mobilization in pursuit  |
   | of the rebirth of the nation"                                  |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | JOB 4: NAME ANYONE ON THE RIGHT                                |
   | STATUS: explicitly criticised by the core account as a modern  |
   | tendency "among the left to label as 'fascist' anybody who     |
   | holds right-wing views"                                        |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

   ----------------------------------------------------------------
   THE OPERATIVE TEST, STATED ONCE AND USED ALL SESSION:
   IS THERE A MASS MOVEMENT MOBILISING FOR NATIONAL REBIRTH?
   yes -> fascism proper (Italy, Germany)
   no, but one-party repression exists -> authoritarian or military
   dictatorship (Japan; Spain and Portugal; Primo de Rivera)
   ----------------------------------------------------------------
```

*The diagram is why "all interwar dictatorships were fascist" is not a simplification but an error. ✅ The core account draws the line itself, in the chapter that introduces Japan and Spain: those regimes had one-party control, imprisonment or death for opponents, secret police and brutal repression, and they were still not fascist states in the strict sense, because they lacked mass mobilisation for national rebirth.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Take the sentence a candidate is most likely to write: "Fascism rose in Italy, Germany and Japan because of the economic crisis." Every clause in it is doing work that has not been justified. It assumes the three cases are instances of one thing. It assumes the thing has a name that fits all three. It assumes a single cause, and it assumes the cause operated the same way in all three places.

Now put the problem the other way round. In 1922 an Italian movement that had won thirty-five seats in a parliament of over five hundred formed a government. In 1933 a German party that had just lost thirty-four seats was invited into a Chancellery. In 1931 a Japanese army acted in Manchuria without asking its own government, and in 1932 naval officers shot the prime minister. ⚠️ Three events, three mechanisms - electoral movement plus threat, electoral decline plus elite intrigue, and military insubordination. If one word is going to cover all three, it has to be shown to cover them, and the showing is the answer.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the most valuable single sentence at the start of any answer on this topic is a definitional one. Not a definition for its own sake - a definition that does work later. If you define fascism by mass mobilisation for national rebirth, then Japan becomes a problem you have to solve rather than a case you can list, and solving it is where the marks are.

### 2. What the first fascist movement itself claimed

✅ **Fact:** the core account sets out six features of the fascism Mussolini came to have in mind. They are worth learning as a set, because the later comparison tests each of them in turn.

| Feature | What the account records | What it becomes in the comparison |
|---|---|---|
| **Stable authoritarian government** | ✅ A reaction to a crisis that made stable democratic government impossible; government would arouse and mobilise the mass of ordinary people and control as many aspects of life as possible, with strong discipline; one aspect was the "corporate state", a separate organisation of workers and employers for each branch of the economy, each with a government official attached | ⚠️ The mobilisation clause is the discriminator. Authoritarian regimes demobilise populations; this one claims to activate them |
| **Extreme nationalism** | ✅ Emphasis on the rebirth of the nation after a period of decline; building the greatness and prestige of the state, with the implication that one's own nation is superior to all others | ⚠️ Rebirth, not restoration. A conservative wants the old order back; this claims to make a new nation |
| **A one-party state** | ✅ No place for democratic debate, because it made decisive government impossible; the cult of the great charismatic leader; Mussolini took the title *il Duce* as Hitler called himself *Fuhrer*; ✅ especially hostile to communism, which explains much of its popularity with big business and the wealthy | ⚠️ Anti-communism is the recruiting device. It is how a movement of ex-servicemen acquires bankers |
| **Economic self-sufficiency (autarky)** | ✅ Vital to the greatness of the state; the government must direct the economic life of the nation, though not in the Marxist sense of state ownership of factories and land | ⚠️ Direction without ownership. This is the formula that keeps industrialists inside the coalition |
| **Modern propaganda** | ✅ Great use of the latest forms - uniforms, marches, songs and displays - to demonstrate that fascists were a completely new and dynamic alternative to the boring, old-fashioned traditional parties, and to mobilise mass support behind the heroic leader | ⚠️ The novelty claim matters. These movements sold themselves as modern, not as reactionary |
| **Military strength and violence** | ✅ An integral part of the fascist way of life; extreme violence against domestic opponents; and ✅ the account notes that the Italian fascists fostered the myth that they had seized power by force, when in fact Mussolini was invited to form a government by the king | ⚠️ Violence is both instrument and self-image, and the self-image outlived the facts |

✅ **Fact:** the account is candid about why this list is loose. Fascism, it says, never produced a great theoretical writer who could explain its philosophies clearly in the way that Marx did for communism, which makes it hard to pin down what was involved; and ✅ Mussolini's constantly changing aims before 1923 suggest that his main concern was simply to acquire power, after which he seems to have improvised his ideas as he went along.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that candour is an examinable asset, not an embarrassment. It licenses a strong opening line - **fascism is better identified by what it does than by what it says**, because what it said changed with circumstance. It also explains why the definitional literature that came later works by listing family features rather than by deriving a doctrine.

### 3. The definitional tests that later scholarship added

⚠️ These are refinements of the same operation, and each adds one usable instrument. They are named here once and then used as tools, not paraded.

| Test | What it adds | Where this session uses it |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Palingenetic ultranationalism** (Roger Griffin) | Makes the myth of national rebirth from decadence the irreducible core, fused with populist ultranationalism | Lesson 13, to explain why an army-led regime with no rebirth-movement is classified differently |
| ✅ **A staged process** (Robert Paxton) | Treats fascism as a sequence rather than a doctrine: intellectual formation, rooting, arrival in power through alliance with traditional elites, exercise of power, then radicalisation or entropy | Lessons 3-5 and 8-9, because the "alliance with traditional elites" stage is exactly what the king and the conservative politicians supplied |
| ✅ **Sacralisation of politics** (Emilio Gentile) | Treats mass ritual, symbol and the cult of the leader as a political religion rather than as decoration | Lesson 14, where the parades and rallies are analysed as machinery rather than pageantry |
| ✅ **A typological cluster** (Stanley Payne) | Sorts the evidence into ideology and goals, style and organisation, and political practice, allowing degrees rather than a yes-or-no verdict | Lesson 16, where the comparison is graded case by case |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the practical value of these four is that they turn a label dispute into a set of questions. Is there a rebirth myth? Was there an elite alliance at the point of entry? Was there a mass liturgy? And on each of style, ideology and practice, how far does the case go? A script that asks those four questions of Japan will produce a better answer than one that asserts a verdict.

### 4. Three words that are not synonyms

This distinction is the working equipment of the whole session, so it is fixed here and used everywhere afterwards.

| Term | Working definition | Interwar instance | The error it prevents |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Fascism** | A mass movement mobilising for national rebirth, which on reaching power builds a party-state around a leader cult | ✅ Italy from 1922; ✅ Germany from 1933 in a radicalised racial form | Using the word for any dictatorship |
| **Authoritarianism** | Rule without political competition, which demobilises rather than mobilises and typically leans on existing institutions - crown, church, army, bureaucracy | ✅ Primo de Rivera in Spain from 1923, whom the core account calls a military dictator but not a fascist; ✅ Franco's post-1939 order; ✅ Salazar's *Estado Novo* in Portugal | Treating conservative dictatorship and mass-mobilising dictatorship as one thing |
| **Military dictatorship** | Rule in which the armed forces, acting as an institution, control or displace civilian government | ✅ Japan after 1932, where the account records that the army more or less ran the country for thirteen years | Assuming that expansionism proves fascism |

✅ **Fact:** the core account applies exactly this discrimination outside Europe as well. Juan Peron in Argentina and Getulio Vargas in Brazil were impressed by Italy and Germany and adopted some fascist ideas, especially the mobilisation of mass support, but they were not really like Mussolini and Hitler; ✅ the account quotes Eric Hobsbawm's formulation that European fascist movements destroyed labour movements while the Latin American leaders they inspired created them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that single contrast is the cleanest proof that the label needs a test rather than an impression. Two regimes can borrow the same style and stand in opposite relations to organised labour. Style travels; function does not.

### 5. What this topic owns, and where it stops

| Material | Owner | What this session may use |
|---|---|---|
| The League, interwar diplomacy, the Abyssinian crisis as an international crisis, the Spanish Civil War as an intervention problem, appeasement | The international-relations owner | ✅ Only the foreign policies of these regimes as evidence about the regimes themselves - the imperial turn as ideology in action, and the popularity of foreign-policy success at home |
| The Russian Revolution, Bolshevism and the Soviet state | The Russian Revolution and USSR owner | ✅ Only the fear of revolution as a political fact inside Italy, Germany and Japan, and the formation of the Italian Communist Party in January 1921 as a dated event |
| The military course of the Second World War; the extermination of Europe's Jews | The Second World War owner | ✅ Nothing beyond the handover stated in Lesson 10; this session ends at the outbreak of war |
| The causes, transmission and instruments of the Great Depression | The world-economy owner | ✅ Only its recorded effects inside these three states, and the recorded counterfactual about its causal weight |
| Italian and German unification; the Meiji Restoration as a modernisation process | The nineteenth-century owners | ✅ Only the inherited weaknesses each state carried into 1918, and the constitutional design Japan carried into the 1930s |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the boundary is not bureaucratic. A "rise of fascism" answer that spends its opening on the Treaty of Versailles is answering a neighbouring question. ✅ One clause is the correct dose - the republic had accepted the treaty and was always associated with defeat and dishonour - and then forward into the regime.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** definitional fastidiousness is an academic luxury. An examiner asking about "the rise of fascism in Italy, Germany and Japan" has already decided that Japan counts, so refusing the label looks like evasion.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection would hold if refusal were the answer. It is not. The disciplined move is to accept the question's framing and then improve it inside the answer: treat the three as members of one interwar authoritarian family, show what they share, and then show that Japan reached the same destination by a different road and without the one feature the core account treats as definitive. ✅ That is exactly what the account does itself - it places Japan in a part of the book titled for the rise of fascism and governments of the right, and then states that Japan was not strictly a fascist state. ⚠️ Following the source's own discrimination is not evasion; it is the demonstration that a candidate has read past the chapter title.

### 7. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** this topic owns one verified General Studies Paper I question in the 2018-2026 span, and it is a definitional-analytical demand rather than a narrative one. ✅ The exact stem, from the official 2021 paper, question 13, is: *"There arose a serious challenge to the Democratic State System between the two World Wars." Evaluate the statement.* ✅ Fifteen marks, to be answered in 250 words.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice what that stem does not ask. It does not ask how Mussolini rose or who Hitler was. It asks about a **system** and about a **challenge** to it, which means the unit of analysis is parliamentary democracy itself and the answer has to explain a failure, not narrate a takeover. Lesson 2 supplies the whole evidence base for that demand, and Lesson 16 supplies the answer architecture.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ What was fascism? Examine its principal features with reference to interwar Europe.
> ⚠️ "Fascism, Nazism and Japanese militarism were three names for one phenomenon." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Distinguish fascism from other forms of interwar authoritarianism.

**Answer-use guidance.** In any of these, define by the mobilisation test in the first two sentences and then apply it as a sorting instrument rather than restating it. ⚠️ Examiners reward the application, not the definition; a definition that is never used again is decoration.

### Traps

- Writing that fascism had a systematic doctrine. ✅ The core account's recorded position is the opposite: no great theoretical writer, changing aims before 1923, and improvisation afterwards.
- Treating "authoritarian", "totalitarian" and "fascist" as interchangeable. They are three different claims with three different evidence requirements.
- Calling Japan fascist without qualification, or calling Franco's Spain a Nazi state.
- Reading the corporate state as a genuine economic doctrine at this stage of the answer. ✅ Its actual operation is assessed in Lesson 6, and the account's verdict there is severe.
- Using "the nation" as the actor. Movements, parties, cabinets, militias and named people act; nations do not.
- Opening a fascism answer with the terms of the Treaty of Versailles.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The six recorded features: authoritarian government with mobilisation, extreme nationalism as rebirth, one-party state with leader cult, autarky, modern propaganda, and violence as a way of life.
2. ✅ Anti-communism is the feature that explains the movements' appeal to business and the wealthy.
3. ✅ The account records no fascist theorist of Marx's stature, and records Mussolini's aims as changing before 1923 and improvised afterwards.
4. ✅ The operative test for the family: mass mobilisation in pursuit of national rebirth.
5. ✅ On that test the account excludes Japan, Spain and Portugal from the strict category while recording their one-party rule, secret police and repression.
6. ✅ Four analytical instruments: rebirth myth (Griffin), staged arrival through elite alliance (Paxton), political religion (Gentile), graded typology (Payne).
7. ✅ Peron and Vargas borrowed the style; Hobsbawm's recorded contrast is that European fascism destroyed labour movements while its Latin American imitators created them.
8. ✅ *il Duce* and *Fuhrer* are the same device in two languages: the leader as the movement's organising principle.
9. ⚠️ Fascism, authoritarianism and military dictatorship differ on one axis above all - whether the population is mobilised or demobilised.
10. ✅ The one owned question, verbatim: *"There arose a serious challenge to the Democratic State System between the two World Wars." Evaluate the statement.* 2021, General Studies Paper I, question 13, 15 marks, 250 words.

### Concept check

**Question:** A script opens: "Fascism was a totalitarian ideology which took power in Italy, Germany and Japan because of the Great Depression." Identify the four errors and rewrite the sentence as an opening worth marks.

**Model answer:** The first error is "ideology". The core account records that fascism produced no theoretical writer comparable to Marx, that Mussolini's aims changed constantly before 1923, and that he improvised afterwards; calling it an ideology imports a coherence the evidence denies. The second error is "totalitarian" used as a description rather than as a claim to be tested: the account's own verdict on Italy is that Mussolini did not create a completely totalitarian system and that authoritarian is the more accurate word, since the king remained head of state and dismissed him in 1943 and the Church remained an alternative focus of loyalty. The third error is treating Japan as a third instance of the same thing: the account states that Japan, Spain and Portugal had many similar features but were not strictly fascist states because they lacked mass mobilisation for national rebirth, and in Japan power was taken by the army acting as an institution, not by a mass party. The fourth error is the single cause: the Depression is decisive for Germany, where the account records that without the economic crisis it is doubtful whether Hitler would have had much chance of attaining power, but Mussolini took office in October 1922, seven years before the Wall Street Crash, and Japan's crisis had a distinct trade-and-silk transmission on top of a constitutional design that already let the army act alone. A marks-earning opening would read: between the wars a family of anti-liberal movements and regimes emerged from a common crisis of parliamentary legitimacy, of which Italy supplied the model and Germany the radicalised racial version; Japan reached an authoritarian destination by a different route, through the institutional autonomy of its armed forces rather than through a mass movement, and the causes therefore have to be weighted case by case rather than asserted in common.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the definitional question is a preliminary to be cleared before the real answer begins. It is the answer's organising instrument. Every later judgement - whether Italy was totalitarian, whether Japan was fascist, whether the three can be compared at all - is settled by the test set here, and a script that never sets one cannot grade anything.

*The next lesson supplies what this one assumed. It has been asserted that a crisis of parliamentary legitimacy was common to all three states. That crisis has not yet been described, and until it is, every national story that follows would be a list of events without a setting.*

## Lesson 2 - The common inheritance: why parliamentary government lost its claim after 1918

Progress: 2/16 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the seven recorded mechanisms of democratic failure; disappointed victory and defeat-as-betrayal; monetary destruction and the ruin of savings; proportional representation and coalition immobilism; the privatisation of political violence; the fear of revolution as a political fact rather than a real prospect; elite defection; and the separate Japanese variant of the same crisis

*The table is the evidence bank for the one owned examination question, and the bottom line is the thesis. ⚠️ Read down the columns and the contrasts appear immediately: mechanism 3 does not apply to Japan at all, which is the first signal that the Japanese case has a different structure; mechanism 1 is strongest in Italy and Germany; mechanism 6 is present everywhere and is the one that actually completes the transfer.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

In 1919 parliamentary democracy looked like the future. Germany held the first completely democratic election in its history in January of that year. Italy introduced votes for all men and proportional representation for its 1919 elections. Japan gave the vote to all adult males in 1925. Within a decade and a half, all three were governed without meaningful parliamentary competition.

The question is what happened in between, and the honest answer is that in none of the three did a fascist army defeat a democratic one. ⚠️ Parliamentary government lost first in the argument about whether it could deliver anything, and only then in the institutions. That sequence is what the examination question is asking about, and it is why an answer built around Mussolini's biography misses the demand.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** think of democratic legitimacy as resting on three promises - security, subsistence and self-respect. The interwar record is of governments that could not keep any of the three, in states where the alternative had already organised itself in the street. The challenge to the democratic state system was therefore not primarily ideological; it was a performance failure that ideology then exploited.

### 2. Unmet expectation: victory that felt like defeat, and defeat rewritten as betrayal

✅ **Fact:** in Italy the grievance was precise and documented. When Italy entered the war the Allies had promised Trentino, the south Tyrol, Istria, Trieste, part of Dalmatia, Adalia, some Aegean islands and a protectorate over Albania. Italy was given the first four areas; the rest went to other states, mainly Yugoslavia, and Albania was to be independent. ✅ Particularly irritating was the failure to get Fiume, which went to Yugoslavia - though the account notes drily that Fiume was not in fact one of the areas that had been promised.

✅ **Fact:** the consequence was a private foreign policy. Gabriele d'Annunzio, a famous romantic poet, marched with a few hundred supporters and occupied Fiume before the Yugoslavs could take it; some army units deserted and supported him with arms and ammunition, and he began to have hopes of overthrowing the government. ✅ He held Fiume for fifteen months until Giovanni Giolitti, prime minister from June 1920, ordered the army to remove him - a risky move, since d'Annunzio was viewed as a national hero. ✅ The army obeyed, d'Annunzio surrendered without a fight, and the government was left highly unpopular.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Fiume episode is the single most compressed piece of evidence in the Italian case, and it is under-used in scripts. In eighteen months an Italian government had watched a poet seize a city with deserting soldiers, had failed to act for over a year, and had finally acted only to be punished for it politically. That is the demonstration of a state that could neither enforce its authority nor afford to.

✅ **Fact:** the German version of unmet expectation was the mirror image. The republic had accepted the humiliating and unpopular Versailles Treaty, with its arms limitations, reparations and war-guilt clause, and was therefore always associated with defeat and dishonour. ✅ Alongside it ran a traditional lack of respect for democratic government and a great admiration for the army and the officer class as the rightful leaders of Germany, and the widespread view that the army had not been defeated but betrayed - stabbed in the back - by democrats who needlessly agreed to the treaty. ✅ The account records the factual refutation: it was General Ludendorff who had asked for an armistice while the Kaiser was still in power, a point most people did not realise, and the legend was eagerly fostered by all enemies of the republic.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the stab-in-the-back legend is the purest example of how a democratic system can be made to lose the argument before it has begun. The republic's founding act - accepting the terms - was recast as its founding crime, and the men who had actually sought the armistice were not the men blamed for it.

### 3. Monetary destruction: what inflation does to a political system

✅ **Fact:** the Italian figures are specific. The government had borrowed heavily, especially from the United States, and the debts had to be repaid; the lira fell from 5 to the dollar in 1914 to 28 in 1921, and the cost of living rose accordingly by at least five times. ✅ There was massive unemployment as heavy industry cut back wartime production, and 2.5 million ex-servicemen had difficulty finding jobs.

✅ **Fact:** the German figures are worse and their political meaning is different. ✅ In 1919 Germany was close to bankruptcy; in August 1921, after paying the £50 million then due, Germany requested permission to suspend payments until the economy recovered, and France refused; in January 1923 French troops occupied the Ruhr, the German government ordered passive resistance, and German industry there was paralysed. ✅ The mark, twenty to the dollar at the end of the war, reached twenty-one billion to the pound by November 1923; a worker paid in notes had to spend them immediately, because by the following day they would be worthless. ✅ Stabilisation came with Gustav Stresemann's new currency, the Rentenmark, in 1924.

✅ **Fact:** and the account is explicit about who lost. The working classes were badly hit as wages failed to keep pace and trade-union funds were wiped out. ✅ Worst affected were the middle classes and small capitalists, who lost their savings, and many began to look towards the Nazis for improvement. ✅ Landowners and industrialists came out of the crisis well, because they still owned their material wealth - farming land, mines and factories - which strengthened the control of big business over the German economy. ✅ The account records that some historians have suggested the inflation was deliberately engineered by wealthy industrialists with that aim, and states plainly that the accusation is impossible to prove one way or the other.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most transferable causal mechanism in the whole topic, and it deserves one sentence in every answer. Inflation does not merely impoverish; it **reallocates**, and it reallocates against exactly the group whose loyalty a parliamentary republic most needs - salaried and saving households with a stake in stability. A wage-earner who loses income can hope for recovery. A household whose savings have been annihilated has had its relationship to the currency, and to the state that issues it, permanently altered.

### 4. Coalition immobilism, and why the electoral system is part of the explanation

✅ **Fact:** in Italy, votes for all men and proportional representation were introduced for the 1919 elections. ✅ The representation was fairer, but it meant a large number of parties: after May 1921 there were at least nine represented - liberals, nationalists, socialists, communists, the Catholic popular party and fascists among them - so no party could win an overall majority and coalitions were inevitable. ✅ Five different cabinets with shaky majorities came and went, and there was growing impatience with a system that seemed designed to prevent decisive government.

✅ **Fact:** the German design failed in the same way and for an additional reason. ✅ Proportional representation produced a Reichstag which in 1928 contained at least eight groups - Social Democrats 153 seats, the German National Party 73, the Catholic Centre 62, the Communists 54, Stresemann's German People's Party 45, the Bavarian People's Party 16 and the National Socialists 12. ✅ Beyond the arithmetic lay an inexperience problem: before 1919 the Reichstag had not controlled policy, the Chancellor had the final authority, and under the new constitution the relation was reversed - yet the parties had not learned the art of compromise. ✅ The communists and nationalists did not believe in democracy at all and refused to support the Social Democrats, and the communist refusal to work with the Social Democrats meant that no strong government of the left was possible.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the two distinct failures inside one word. "Fragmentation" is arithmetic - too many parties for a majority. "Immobilism" is behavioural - parties that could have combined refused to. The second is the more damaging, because it is a choice rather than a design fault, and it recurs exactly in Italy, where the socialists would not work with Giolitti to curb fascist violence.

### 5. Armed politics: when private force becomes normal

✅ **Fact:** the German record of privatised violence is dense and dated. ✅ In January 1919 the communists attempted to seize power in the Spartacist Rising, led by Karl Liebknecht and Rosa Luxemburg, occupying almost every major city; President Ebert was besieged in the Chancellery, and the government defeated them only by accepting the help of the *Freikorps*, independent volunteer regiments raised by anti-communist ex-army officers. ✅ The account calls this a sign of the government's weakness - it had to depend on private forces it did not control - and records that the two communist leaders did not receive a fair trial but were clubbed to death by *Freikorps* members. ✅ In March 1920 right-wing groups attempted the Kapp Putsch when the government tried to disband the *Freikorps*: Berlin was occupied, the cabinet fled to Dresden, the army took no action because the generals sympathised with the political right, and the Putsch was defeated by a general strike of Berlin workers. ✅ Nobody was punished except Kapp. ✅ A series of political assassinations followed, mainly by ex-*Freikorps* members, the victims including Foreign Minister Walther Rathenau and the armistice delegation's leader; ✅ the courts let right-wing offenders off lightly, and throughout Germany the legal and teaching professions, the civil service and the army tended to be anti-republican.

✅ **Fact:** in November 1923 Hitler, helped by Ludendorff, attempted to take control of the Bavarian state government in Munich and then lead a national revolution; the police easily broke up the march. ✅ He was sentenced to five years and served nine months, because the Bavarian authorities had some sympathy with his aims.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sentencing pattern is the mechanism, not the anecdote. When the courts punish one side of a civil conflict and excuse the other, the state has stopped being an arbiter and has become a participant. ⚠️ A republic whose judges, officials and officers are hostile to it is not being attacked from outside; it is being hollowed out from inside, and this is the single most precise sense in which the democratic state system was challenged.

### 6. The fear of revolution - a political fact that was not an accurate forecast

✅ **Fact:** the Italian sequence is instructive because the account dates the fear and the reality separately. ✅ Industrialisation after unification had produced a strong socialist party and trade unions, and in 1919 and 1920 they organised a wave of strikes accompanied by rioting, looting of shops and occupation of factories; in Turin, factory councils reminiscent of the Russian soviets appeared, and in the south socialist leagues of farmworkers seized land from wealthy landowners and set up co-operatives. ✅ The government's prestige sank because it failed to protect property, and many property-owners were convinced a left-wing revolution was at hand, especially after the Italian Communist Party was formed in January 1921.

✅ **Fact:** and then the crucial qualification. ✅ By then the chances of revolution were receding: the strikes and factory occupations were fizzling out, because although workers tried to maintain production and claim control of the factories, it proved impossible - suppliers refused them raw materials and they needed engineers and managers. ✅ The formation of the Communist Party actually made revolution less likely because it split the forces of the left. ✅ Nevertheless the fear of revolution remained strong.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most under-used sentence in the Italian evidence, and it converts a narrative into an argument. **The fascist movement was not bought as insurance against a revolution that was coming; it was bought as insurance against a revolution that had already failed.** The fear outlived its object, and it was the fear, not the danger, that was politically operative. ⚠️ Any answer that can state that distinction has demonstrated analysis rather than recall.

### 7. Elite defection, and the weakness of the defence

✅ **Fact:** in Italy the defection is traceable in stages. ✅ After the fascists won no seats in 1919 and the factory occupations failed, Mussolini came out as the defender of private enterprise and property, attracting much-needed financial support from wealthy business interests. ✅ By the end of 1921, though his programme was vague in the extreme, he had gained the support of property-owners in general as a guarantee of law and order. ✅ He then made conciliatory speeches about the Catholic Church, and Pope Pius XI swung the Church into line behind him, seeing him as a good anti-communist weapon. ✅ When he announced in September 1922 that he had dropped the republican part of his programme, even the king began to look more favourably on the fascists.

✅ **Fact:** the opposition, meanwhile, did not combine. ✅ The anti-fascist groups failed to co-operate and made no determined efforts to keep the fascists out; the communists refused to co-operate with the socialists; ✅ Giolitti held the May 1921 elections hoping the fascists would win some seats and then support his government, and was willing to overlook their violence, believing they would become more responsible inside parliament. ✅ They won thirty-five seats while the socialists took 123 - and the account's verdict is blunt: clearly there should have been no question of a fascist takeover. ✅ The socialists must take much of the blame for refusing to work with the government to curb fascist violence, since a coalition of Giolitti's nationalist bloc and the socialists could have made a reasonably stable government, excluding the fascists. ✅ They would not co-operate, and Giolitti resigned in exasperation and despair.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that paragraph is the whole "challenge to the democratic state system" argument in one national case. In May 1921 the parliamentary arithmetic was overwhelmingly against fascism. Eighteen months later fascism held the premiership. Nothing changed in the arithmetic; what changed was the willingness of the people inside the system to use it.

### 8. The Japanese variant of the same crisis

✅ **Fact:** Japan's version substitutes different components into the same equation. ✅ Democracy was relatively new - the constitution was formally accepted in 1889, the first Diet met in 1890, and all adult males received the vote in 1925. ✅ After the First World War the elite groups became critical: especially troublesome were the army and the conservatives, strongly entrenched in the House of Peers and the Privy Council, who seized every opportunity to discredit the government. ✅ They criticised Foreign Minister Shidehara Kijuro for his conciliatory approach to China and were strong enough to bring the government down in 1927 and reverse his policy.

✅ **Fact:** corruption did the rest of the delegitimising work. ✅ Many politicians were corrupt and regularly accepted bribes from big business; sometimes fighting broke out in the Diet as charges of corruption were flung about; the system no longer inspired respect and the prestige of parliament suffered. ✅ Economically, the great trading boom of the war years lasted only until the middle of 1921, when Europe began to recover lost markets; unemployment and industrial unrest developed, and farmers were hit by rapidly falling rice prices after bumper harvests. ✅ When farmers and industrial workers tried to organise a political party they were ruthlessly suppressed by the police - so workers as well as the army and the right became hostile to a parliament that posed as democratic, allowed the left to be suppressed, and accepted bribes from big business.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Japanese case adds a mechanism the European cases lack, and it is worth a sentence of its own in a comparative answer. In Italy and Germany parliamentary government lost the right and kept part of the left. In Japan it lost both simultaneously, because it was seen as corrupt by the right and as repressive by the left. ⚠️ A system attacked from two directions at once has no defenders at all, which is one reason no organised civilian resistance appeared when the army moved.

### 9. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** this lesson makes democratic failure look overdetermined. If seven mechanisms operated in all three states, why did the same mechanisms not destroy parliamentary government in Britain, France or the Netherlands, which also faced war debt, unemployment and the Depression?

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is correct and it sharpens the thesis rather than defeating it. The mechanisms are necessary conditions, not sufficient ones, and the difference lies in mechanisms 5, 6 and 7 rather than in 1 to 4. ✅ Britain and France had economic distress without a credible revolutionary threat of the Italian kind, without judiciaries and officer corps hostile to the constitution, and without governing elites who preferred an authoritarian alternative. ⚠️ The usable formulation is therefore causal and conditional: **economic and military humiliation supplied the demand for an alternative; only elite defection converted that demand into a transfer of power.** That sentence is worth learning, because it is the difference between a list and an argument.

### 10. UPSC application

✅ **Fact:** this lesson is the direct evidence base for the single owned question: *"There arose a serious challenge to the Democratic State System between the two World Wars." Evaluate the statement.*

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the directive is *evaluate*, which requires a judgement on the strength of the proposition, not a survey. The strongest available judgement is that the proposition is correct but incomplete - correct that the challenge was serious and general; incomplete because the word "challenge" suggests an external attack, whereas the evidence shows internal abandonment. ⚠️ The answer that scores is the one that accepts the statement, then improves it: the challenge came from three directions at once - mass movements below, conservative elites above, and the constitutional mechanisms of the democracies themselves.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Account for the collapse of parliamentary democracy in interwar Europe.
> ⚠️ "Fascism did not defeat democracy; it occupied the space democracy vacated." Comment.
> ⚠️ Examine the economic roots of interwar authoritarianism.

**Answer-use guidance.** Use the mechanisms as an ordered spine - expectation, money, immobilism, armed politics, fear, elite defection, weak opposition - and put named evidence against at least four of them. ⚠️ The examiner is looking for the transition from mechanism to case, so one Italian, one German and one Japanese instance should appear before the halfway point.

### Traps

- Writing that democracy "was overthrown". ✅ In Italy the king invited Mussolini; in Germany Hindenburg appointed Hitler; in Japan the constitution was never abolished.
- Treating the fear of revolution as evidence that revolution was imminent. ✅ The account records that the chances were receding and that the communist split made revolution less likely.
- Blaming proportional representation alone. Fragmentation is arithmetic; the refusal of parties to combine is a choice, and the account blames the Italian socialists and the German communists by name.
- Giving hyperinflation as a German experience only. ✅ Italy's cost of living rose at least fivefold between 1914 and 1921.
- Forgetting that Japan's parliamentary crisis predates the Depression: ✅ the trade boom ended in mid-1921, and the 1927 China-policy reversal came before the Wall Street Crash.
- Describing any of this as a national characteristic rather than as a set of decisions by identifiable institutions and people.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Italy's promised gains: Trentino, south Tyrol, Istria, Trieste, part of Dalmatia, Adalia, Aegean islands, Albanian protectorate - first four delivered; Fiume, never promised, became the grievance.
2. ✅ D'Annunzio held Fiume for fifteen months; Giolitti ordered the army in June 1920; the government was left unpopular for enforcing its own authority.
3. ✅ Lira 5 to the dollar in 1914, 28 in 1921; cost of living up at least fivefold; 2.5 million ex-servicemen seeking work.
4. ✅ Mark 20 to the dollar at the war's end; twenty-one billion to the pound by November 1923; Rentenmark 1924.
5. ✅ Inflation's political incidence: unions' funds wiped out, middle-class savings destroyed, landowners and industrialists protected by real assets.
6. ✅ Italy: at least nine parties after May 1921, five cabinets 1919-22. Germany: at least eight groups in 1928, with the communists refusing to combine with the Social Democrats.
7. ✅ German armed politics: Spartacist rising January 1919, *Freikorps* dependence, Kapp Putsch March 1920, Rathenau's assassination, Beer-Hall Putsch November 1923 punished with nine months served.
8. ✅ The receding-revolution point: occupations failed because suppliers withheld materials and managers were needed; the communist split reduced the danger; the fear persisted.
9. ✅ Elite defection in Italy: business money after the 1919 failure, property-owners by late 1921, Pius XI as an anti-communist calculation, the king after the republican plank was dropped in September 1922.
10. ✅ Japan: elite hostility in the Peers and Privy Council; the army forced out the Shidehara China policy in 1927; corruption discredited the Diet; the trade boom ended in mid-1921; worker parties were suppressed by the police.
11. ⚠️ Necessary versus sufficient: distress created demand; elite defection completed the transfer.
12. ⚠️ Verdict line for the owned question: the interwar democracies were not stormed but abandoned - by electorates that lost confidence and by elites who preferred authoritarian order to democratic risk.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two states experience severe post-war inflation, coalition instability and political violence. One keeps parliamentary government and one does not. Using the evidence in this lesson, identify which mechanism makes the difference and defend the choice.

**Model answer:** The decisive mechanism is elite defection - the sixth - because it is the only one that actually transfers power, and the Italian evidence isolates it almost experimentally. In May 1921 the fascists held thirty-five seats against 123 socialist seats, and the account states plainly that there should have been no question of a fascist takeover; the arithmetic was against them. Eighteen months later Mussolini was prime minister. Between those two points the distribution of seats did not move in his favour; what moved was the behaviour of people already inside the system. Wealthy business interests funded him once he defended private property; property-owners in general backed him by the end of 1921 as a guarantee of order; Pius XI swung the Church behind him as an anti-communist weapon; the king looked on him more favourably once the republican plank was dropped in September 1922 and then refused to declare a state of emergency. The same mechanism completes the German case, where Papen and Schleicher persuaded Hindenburg to appoint Hitler in January 1933 after the Nazis had lost thirty-four seats and about two million votes in November 1932, and where the courts and the civil service had already withheld their loyalty from the republic. The first five mechanisms explain why an authoritarian alternative found a market: they generate demand. They do not explain supply. The seventh, weak opposition, is the permissive condition that allows defection to go unpunished - the Italian socialists refusing Giolitti, the German communists refusing the Social Democrats. So the answer is that distress makes a democracy unpopular, while elite defection makes it defunct.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that a list of causes is a causal explanation. Seven mechanisms operated in France and Britain too, in weaker form, without producing a dictatorship. An examination answer earns its marks by ranking the mechanisms and naming the one that converts a crisis of confidence into a change of regime, not by proving that many bad things happened at once.

*With the common setting established, the session turns to the case in which every mechanism can be watched operating in sequence. Italy is taught first not because it came first in time alone, but because it is the only case in which a movement built itself from nothing into a government in thirty-six months, in public, with the evidence recorded at each step.*

## Lesson 3 - Italy 1919-22: the movement, the squads, and the manufacture of an alternative

Progress: 3/16 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the *fasci di combattimento* of 1919 and their socialist-republican programme; the Biennio Rosso of 1919-20 and why the occupations failed; the electoral failure of 1919 and the turn to property; squadrismo from late 1920 and the local *ras*; the May 1921 result; the Church, big business and the king; the failed general strike of summer 1922
