```text
   |   CENT of government spending in 1938-9                       |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+

   THE TWO VERDICTS
   +------------------------------+--------------------------------+
   | SUCCESSFUL                   | ONLY SUPERFICIALLY SUCCESSFUL  |
   | benefits delivered; a        | the "economic miracle" an      |
   | flourishing economy; great   | illusion - a huge budget       |
   | popularity into the 1940s;   | deficit, the country           |
   | "if only Hitler had kept     | technically bankrupt           |
   | Germany out of war, all      | full employment bought with a  |
   | would have been well"        | brutal anti-Jewish campaign    |
   |                              | and massive rearmament         |
   |                              | autarky impossible without     |
   |                              | seizing eastern Europe         |
   |                              | -> "permanent success depended |
   |                              | on success in war"             |
   |                              | Evans: no marked improvement   |
   |                              | for most middle-class wage-    |
   |                              | earners 1933-39; per-capita    |
   |                              | consumption of many basic      |
   |                              | foodstuffs DECLINED in the     |
   |                              | mid-1930s; wage gains came     |
   |                              | only through longer hours      |
   +------------------------------+--------------------------------+
```

*⚠️ The visual is deliberately built so that the components of the recovery sit directly above the verdicts. Read them together and the argument writes itself: two of the four routes to full employment were military and a third was the confiscation of other people's jobs, which is why the second verdict does not merely disagree with the first but explains it.*

### 1. The achievement, stated at full strength before it is qualified

✅ **Fact:** Hitler was successful in eliminating unemployment, and the account calls this probably the most important reason for his popularity with ordinary people. ✅ When he came to power the figure still stood at over six million; by the end of 1935 it had dropped to just over two million, and by 1939 it was negligible.

✅ **Fact:** the account is equally clear that terror alone is the wrong model. ✅ It would be wrong to give the impression that Hitler hung on to power simply by terrorising the entire nation: if you were a Jew, a communist or a socialist, or if you persisted in protesting and criticising, you would run into trouble, but many people who had no great interest in politics could usually live quite happily under the Nazis, because Hitler took care to please many important groups in society. ✅ Even as late as 1943, when the fortunes of war had turned, he somehow retained his popularity with ordinary people. ✅ Gotz Aly argues that the Nazis were as much socialist as nationalist and genuinely tried to make life better for ordinary Germans, and that Hitler told a reporter his ambition was to raise the general standard of living and make the German people rich.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a serious answer states the achievement at full strength before qualifying it, and for a specific reason. ⚠️ The regime's durability is an explanandum: if the record were uniformly bad, the support would be inexplicable and the only remaining explanation would be terror, which Lesson 10's Gestapo figure has already shown to be insufficient. ⚠️ **The material record is the missing variable in any consent-based explanation**, which is why it has to be presented honestly and then interrogated.

### 2. How the unemployment figure was actually reduced

✅ **Fact:** the account lists the routes. ✅ Public works schemes provided thousands of extra jobs. ✅ A large party bureaucracy was set up as the party expanded rapidly, providing thousands of extra office and administrative posts. ✅ There were purges of Jews and anti-Nazis from the civil service and from many other jobs connected with law, education, journalism, broadcasting, the theatre and music, leaving large numbers of vacancies. ✅ Conscription was reintroduced in 1935. ✅ Rearmament was started in 1934 and gradually speeded up. ✅ Thus Hitler had provided what the unemployed had been demanding in their marches in 1932: work and bread.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single most important analytical passage in the German economic record, and it should be written out rather than summarised in an answer. ⚠️ Of the five routes, one is ordinary counter-cyclical public investment; one is the expansion of a party apparatus; two - conscription and rearmament - remove men from the labour market and place them on the state's payroll for military purposes; and one is the transfer of existing jobs from people expelled on racial and political grounds. ⚠️ **Persecution was not a separate policy running alongside the employment programme; it was one of its instruments.** That sentence is the highest-value line available on this material, and it is the reason the economic record cannot be assessed as if it were morally separable from the rest of the regime.

### 3. The Four-Year Plan and the shape of the war economy

✅ **Fact:** the Nazis had no special ideas about the economy, unlike the communists, but they did have basic aims: to eliminate unemployment and to make Germany self-sufficient by boosting exports and reducing imports, a policy known as autarky. ✅ The idea was to put the economy onto a war footing so that all materials necessary for waging war could be produced as far as possible in Germany itself, ensuring that Germany would never again be hamstrung by a trade blockade like the one imposed by the Allies during the First World War. ✅ The centrepiece was the Four-Year Plan introduced in 1936 under Hermann Goering, head of the Luftwaffe, and its policies are listed in the visual above. ✅ An earlier trade and payments programme directed by Hjalmar Schacht had already redirected German commerce through bilateral arrangements before the Plan formalised the system.

✅ **Fact:** the military budget accounted for fifty-two per cent of government spending in 1938-9, which the account calls an incredible amount for peacetime. ✅ Richard Overy is quoted to the effect that this stemmed from Hitler's desire to turn Germany into an economic and military superpower before the rest of the world caught up.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the argumentative structure of the blockade rationale, because it is a rare case where a regime's stated aim and its structural predicament coincide. ⚠️ Autarky was sought because of a remembered blockade; autarky was unattainable within Germany's borders; therefore the pursuit of autarky generated a requirement for territory. ⚠️ That is not hindsight but the account's own reasoning in the verdict section below, and it is what makes the economic argument continuous with the foreign-policy one rather than separate from it.

### 4. The two verdicts, weighed

✅ **Fact:** one school claims the Nazis were successful up to 1939 because they provided many benefits and developed a flourishing economy - hence Hitler's popularity with the masses, which endured well into the 1940s; on this theory, if only he had kept Germany out of war, all would have been well and the Third Reich might have lasted as he boasted.

✅ **Fact:** the opposing view is that the successes were superficial and could not stand the test of time. ✅ The so-called economic miracle was an illusion: there was a huge budget deficit and the country was technically bankrupt. ✅ Even the superficial success was achieved by methods unacceptable in a modern civilised society: full employment only at the cost of a brutal anti-Jewish campaign and a massive rearmament programme; self-sufficiency impossible unless Germany could take over and exploit large areas of eastern Europe belonging to Poland, Czechoslovakia and Russia; ✅ and therefore permanent success depended on success in war, so there was no possibility of Hitler keeping out of war. ✅ Nor was there much evidence of improvement in ordinary living standards: Richard Evans is quoted to the effect that most statistical investigations agree that the economic situation of the majority of middle-class wage-earners did not markedly improve between 1933 and 1939; ✅ as concentration on rearmament increased there were shortages of food and other important goods, and per-capita consumption of many basic foodstuffs declined in the mid-1930s; ✅ any wage increases came about only through working longer hours.

✅ **Fact:** the account's conclusion is Alan Bullock's: recognition of the benefits Hitler's rule brought to Germany needs to be tempered by the realisation that for the Fuhrer, and for a considerable section of the German people, these were by-products of his true purpose - the creation of an instrument of power with which to realise a policy of expansion that in the end was to admit no limits.

✅ **Fact:** and even the policy of preparedness failed. ✅ Hitler's plans were designed to be completed in the early 1940s, probably around 1942; in 1939 the economy was not ready for a major war, although it was strong enough to defeat Poland and France. ✅ Overy notes that the large programmes of war production were not yet complete and some were barely started, and that Hitler later reflected that militarisation had been mismanaged. ✅ Adam Tooze argues that Hitler resisted pressure from advisers to prepare for a long war because he believed Germany had no chance of winning one; ✅ in the first year of the war most increased military expenditure went on aircraft, artillery and ammunition for a western war expected to be fairly short, with preparations for the attack on Russia to follow.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second verdict does not merely rebut the first; it **absorbs** it, and that is how an examination answer should deploy them. ⚠️ The benefits were real, which is why support was real; the benefits were produced by instruments that required war, which is why the "if only he had stayed out of war" counterfactual is incoherent. ⚠️ A regime that reaches full employment through conscription, rearmament and the confiscation of expelled people's jobs, while running a huge deficit and pursuing an autarky its borders cannot supply, has not built a peacetime economy that a war then interrupted. ⚠️ The one-sentence verdict: **the Nazi economic record was a genuine short-run success purchased with instruments that made a long-run peace impossible.**

### 5. The foreign-policy dividend

✅ **Fact:** the account is direct about this as a source of domestic strength. ✅ Hitler's foreign policy was a brilliant success, and with each successive triumph more and more Germans began to think of him as infallible. ✅ His achievements in foreign affairs were extremely popular: with each new success - the announcement of rearmament, the remilitarisation of the Rhineland, the union with Austria and the incorporation of Czechoslovakia into the Reich - it seemed that Germany was reasserting its rightful position as a great power. ✅ This was where Goebbels's propaganda probably had its greatest impact, building Hitler's image as the charismatic and infallible figure destined to restore the country's greatness. ✅ Even though there was little enthusiasm for war, his popularity reached new heights in the summer of 1940 with the rapid defeat of France.

⚠️ **Boundary note.** ⚠️ The diplomacy of those episodes - how they were achieved, what the other powers did and why they did not resist - belongs to the international-relations owner and is not taught here. ⚠️ What belongs here is the domestic political function: foreign-policy success was the regime's second product after employment, and it was the one for which the propaganda apparatus was most effective.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this produces the regime's characteristic dynamic, and it is worth stating because it applies to Italy too. ⚠️ A regime that has made national restoration its legitimating claim must keep restoring, because the supply of grievances is finite and each success raises the threshold for the next. ✅ Note the observation the account makes in the Italian case: little enthusiasm for European war and conquest had been generated despite all the propaganda about reviving the glories of Rome. ⚠️ Both regimes found that foreign-policy triumphs were popular and that the wars they eventually produced were not.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the "superficially successful" verdict relies on counterfactual reasoning - that autarky would have been impossible without conquest, and that the deficit was unsustainable. Neither was tested, because the war intervened. A record judged against an imagined peacetime future is not being judged at all.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection has force against loose versions of the claim and none against the evidence actually held. ⚠️ Three of the four elements of the second verdict are observational rather than counterfactual: the composition of the employment recovery is recorded; the military budget at fifty-two per cent of government spending in 1938-9 is recorded; and the consumption and wage findings reported through Evans are recorded. ⚠️ Only the autarky argument is counterfactual, and it is the account's own and is supported by the resource logic. ⚠️ The honest formulation concedes the point and keeps the conclusion: **the record up to 1939 is judged on what it was made of, not on what it might have become.**

### 7. UPSC application

⚠️ This lesson holds the material for the "domestic success" family of questions, which reward candidates who can assess rather than list.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The Nazi economic recovery was a preparation for war rather than a programme for prosperity." Examine.
> ⚠️ How successful was Hitler in domestic affairs between 1933 and 1939?
> ⚠️ Account for the popularity of the Nazi regime in the 1930s.

**Answer-use guidance.** For the second framing, the structure that scores is components before verdicts: give the unemployment fall from over six million to just over two million by the end of 1935 and negligible by 1939, then name the five routes, then let the second verdict do its work with the fifty-two per cent figure and the Evans findings on consumption and wages, and close with Bullock. ⚠️ Avoid the trap of a balanced list with no judgement; the directive words in this family almost always require a verdict.

### Traps

- Presenting full employment as a straightforward policy success. ✅ Two of the five routes were military and one was the confiscation of jobs from expelled people.
- Omitting that the country was running a huge deficit and was technically bankrupt.
- Saying living standards rose. ✅ Evans's recorded finding is no marked improvement for most middle-class wage-earners, declining per-capita consumption of many basic foodstuffs in the mid-1930s, and wage gains only through longer hours.
- Saying Germany achieved autarky. ✅ The account's point is that it could not, without seizing territory in eastern Europe.
- Saying Germany was fully prepared for war in 1939. ✅ Plans were designed for the early 1940s; Overy records incomplete and barely started programmes; Tooze records deliberate avoidance of long-war preparation.
- Treating foreign-policy success as a diplomatic matter only. Domestically it was the second product of the regime and the main achievement of its propaganda.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Unemployment over six million in January 1933, just over two million by the end of 1935, negligible by 1939.
2. ✅ Five routes: public works; the expanding party bureaucracy; purges creating vacancies; conscription from 1935; rearmament from 1934.
3. ✅ Nazi economic aims: eliminate unemployment and achieve autarky, putting the economy on a war footing so a blockade like that of the First World War could never again be decisive.
4. ✅ Four-Year Plan 1936 under Goering: production directives, labour direction, agricultural yields, price and rent control, exchange-rate manipulation, public works, forced bilateral trade and blocked accounts, synthetic rubber and wool and petrol from coal, and increased armaments spending.
5. ✅ Military budget fifty-two per cent of government spending in 1938-9; Overy's comment on superpower ambition.
6. ✅ First verdict: genuine benefits, a flourishing economy, durable popularity, and the claim that peace would have preserved it.
7. ✅ Second verdict: an illusory miracle, a huge deficit and technical bankruptcy; full employment at the cost of persecution and rearmament; autarky impossible without eastern conquest; therefore permanent success depended on war.
8. ✅ Evans: no marked improvement for most middle-class wage-earners 1933-39; declining per-capita consumption of many basic foodstuffs in the mid-1930s; wage gains only through longer hours.
9. ✅ Bullock: the benefits were by-products of the true purpose, an instrument of power for unlimited expansion.
10. ✅ Preparedness: plans aimed at the early 1940s; not ready in 1939 though strong enough for Poland and France; Overy on incomplete programmes and mismanaged militarisation; Tooze on the deliberate avoidance of long-war preparation.
11. ✅ Foreign-policy triumphs made many think Hitler infallible; Goebbels's propaganda had its greatest impact here; popularity peaked in summer 1940.
12. ⚠️ Comparative line: Italy never eliminated unemployment or achieved self-sufficiency; Germany eliminated unemployment and still never achieved autarky.

### Concept check

**Question:** Evaluate the proposition that "if Hitler had kept Germany out of war, the Nazi regime would have been a domestic success". Use only the evidence in this lesson.

**Model answer:** The proposition is incoherent rather than merely wrong, because the instruments that produced the domestic record were the same instruments that made war unavoidable. Take the record first. Unemployment fell from over six million in January 1933 to just over two million by the end of 1935 and to negligible levels by 1939, and the account calls this probably the most important reason for Hitler's popularity. But the recorded routes to that outcome were public works, an expanding party bureaucracy, the vacancies created by purging Jews and anti-Nazis from the civil service, law, education, journalism, broadcasting, theatre and music, conscription reintroduced in 1935, and rearmament begun in 1934. Two of those are military and one is racial persecution, so the employment achievement cannot be detached from either war preparation or exclusion. The fiscal position reinforces the point: the military budget took fifty-two per cent of government spending in 1938-9, there was a huge budget deficit and the country was technically bankrupt. The autarky aim completes it: self-sufficiency was unattainable unless Germany took over and exploited large areas of eastern Europe belonging to Poland, Czechoslovakia and Russia, so permanent success depended on success in war and there was no possibility of staying out. Finally the claimed prosperity is itself doubtful, since Evans records no marked improvement for most middle-class wage-earners between 1933 and 1939, declining per-capita consumption of many basic foodstuffs in the mid-1930s, and wage gains obtained only by working longer hours. Bullock's conclusion is the right ending: the benefits were by-products of the creation of an instrument of power for a policy of expansion that in the end admitted no limits. A counterfactual peace would therefore not have preserved the achievement; it would have removed the mechanism that produced it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the economic record as a morally separable ledger that can be praised on its own terms and condemned elsewhere. The account's own list makes the separation impossible, because the vacancies created by persecution appear as a component of the employment recovery. An answer that keeps the two apart has not read its own evidence.

*Germany is now complete: inheritance, collapse, appointment, consolidation, racial state and economy. The session turns to the case where none of this machinery existed - no mass party, no leader cult, no coordination, no party-state - and where a comparable authoritarian outcome was nevertheless reached.*

## Lesson 12 - Japan: the constitutional design that made army autonomy possible

Progress: 12/16 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the opening of Japan and the humiliation of the unequal treaties; the Meiji settlement of 1868 and the German-modelled constitution of 1889; the emperor's powers and the Diet's one advantage; the wars of 1894-95 and 1904-5 and the logic of neighbouring conquest; the gains of 1914-19; the elite institutions outside parliamentary control; and why the Japanese story must start with institutions rather than with a movement

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's account of Commodore Perry's arrival in 1853 and the treaties regarded as a national humiliation; the Meiji Restoration of 1868 and the programme of railways, roads, cotton and silk industry and a parliamentary system modelled on Germany's constitution; the colonisation of Korea and Manchuria and the wars with China and Russia; the 1902 alliance with Britain and the seizure of German colonies in China; "Big Five" status; and the constitutional description of the emperor's powers and the Diet's ability to initiate laws
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage sits in Lesson 10
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the machinery, with the points parliament could not reach marked

```text
   THE DESIGN OF 1889 - A REPRESENTATIVE SYSTEM WITH RESERVED AREAS
   ==================================================================

                        THE EMPEROR
        "sacred and inviolable"; could DISSOLVE THE DIET whenever
        he felt like it; took decisions about WAR AND PEACE; was
        COMMANDER-IN-CHIEF OF THE ARMED FORCES
                              |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
        |                     |                     |
   HOUSE OF PEERS        CABINET OF           PRIVY COUNCIL
   appointed             MINISTERS            function: to interpret
   [OUTSIDE ELECTORAL    appointed BY THE     and safeguard the
    CONTROL]             EMPEROR              constitution
                        [NOT chosen by the    [OUTSIDE ELECTORAL
                         Diet]                 CONTROL]
        |                     |                     |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
                              |
                        THE DIET
               elected LOWER house; first elections
               and first meeting 1890; ALL ADULT MALES
               given the vote in 1925
               ITS ONE GREAT ADVANTAGE: "it could initiate
               new laws", so the cabinet found it was not as
               susceptible to their will as expected
   ==================================================================

   WHERE THE ARMED FORCES SIT IN THIS PICTURE
   +----------------------------------------------------------------+
   | the emperor is commander-in-chief -> the chain of military      |
   | command does NOT run through the cabinet or the Diet            |
   | the army and the conservatives are "strongly entrenched" in the |
   | House of Peers and the Privy Council                            |
   | => THE ARMY HAS AN INSTITUTIONAL POSITION THAT NO ELECTION CAN  |
   |    ALTER AND NO CABINET CAN DISCIPLINE                          |
   +----------------------------------------------------------------+

   CONTRAST WITH THE TWO EUROPEAN CASES
   ITALY / GERMANY: a MOVEMENT is built outside the state, wins mass
      support, and is then admitted to the state by a head of state
   JAPAN: NO movement is needed. The instrument of authoritarian rule
      is ALREADY INSIDE the constitution, is already armed, and is
      already outside civilian control
```

*⚠️ This is the lesson's whole argument in a diagram, and it is why Japan is taught through institutions rather than through a narrative of crisis. In Italy and Germany an outside force had to be let in. In Japan nothing had to be let in, because the decisive actor was already inside the constitution and had never been subordinated to the elected chamber.*

### 1. The starting point: modernisation as a response to humiliation

✅ **Fact:** Japan's close contact with the West dated from 1853, when the American Commodore Matthew Perry sailed into Yokohama harbour with four battleships and demanded that Japan open up trade with the United States. ✅ Over the next five years Japan had little choice but to sign trade treaties with several Western countries, and the signing of these treaties was regarded by the Japanese as a great national humiliation. ✅ Gradually a determination to modernise and strengthen the country developed.

✅ **Fact:** beginning in 1868 with the restoration of the Meiji emperor, the Japanese embarked on a policy of building railways, improving the road system, starting modern industries like cotton and silk manufacture, and introducing a more democratic parliamentary system modelled on Germany's constitution. ✅ For the first time in over two and a half centuries Japan became a unified and centralised empire.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two features of that origin shaped everything afterwards, and both are examinable. ⚠️ First, modernisation was **defensive in motive**: its purpose was to avoid the fate the treaties foreshadowed, which meant that military capacity was not one goal among many but the point of the exercise. ⚠️ Second, the constitutional model chosen was **imperial German**, which is to say a model in which an elected chamber existed alongside a monarch who retained command of the armed forces and appointed ministers. ⚠️ That choice is the institutional origin of the 1930s crisis, and it is exactly the arrangement that Lesson 7 showed Weimar reversing in Germany itself after 1919 - while Japan kept it.

### 2. The logic of expansion, built in from the start

✅ **Fact:** the government decided that the best way to prevent the Western powers from treating Japan as they treated China was to occupy neighbouring territories: first Korea and then Manchuria were colonised, which caused two wars, first with China in 1894-95 and then with Russia in 1904-5. ✅ Japan was victorious in both; in the case of Russia this was the first time an Asian country had defeated one of the European great powers, and it meant Japan was now the dominant power in the Far East.

✅ **Fact:** a military alliance had already been signed with Britain in 1902, and when war broke out in 1914 Japan entered on the British side; the main contribution was to seize German colonies and bases in China. ✅ Japan was represented at the Versailles conference in 1919, became a member of the League of Nations and was officially recognised as one of the "Big Five" world powers, with a powerful navy, a well-trained and well-equipped army and a great deal of influence in China.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence 1894, 1904, 1914 matters for the comparison because it establishes that expansion in Japan **preceded** the interwar crisis by decades and was a policy of governments that were not authoritarian in the 1930s sense. ⚠️ This is the strongest single objection to reading Japanese expansion in the 1930s as the product of a fascist ideology: the policy predates the crisis, and what changed after 1931 was not the objective but who decided it and by what means. ⚠️ The usable formulation: **in Japan the 1930s changed the control of foreign policy, not its direction.**

### 3. The system as it actually operated

✅ **Fact:** it was during the 1880s that the emperor gave way to growing demands for a national assembly, in the belief that constitutions and representative government had made the United States and western Europe successful. ✅ Gradually a more representative system was introduced consisting of a house of appointed peers, a cabinet of ministers appointed by the emperor, and a Privy Council whose function was to interpret and safeguard the new constitution, formally accepted in 1889. ✅ It provided for an elected lower house, the Diet; the first elections were held and the Diet met in 1890.

✅ **Fact:** however, the system was far from democratic and the emperor retained enormous power: he could dissolve the Diet whenever he felt like it, took decisions about war and peace, was commander-in-chief of the armed forces, and was regarded as sacred and inviolable. ✅ The doctrine drawn from that command power - the **independence of the supreme command** - held that military advice and orders reached the throne outside the cabinet altogether. ✅ But the Diet had one great advantage: it could initiate new laws, and consequently the cabinet found that it was not as susceptible to their will as they had expected.

✅ **Fact:** politically the course seemed set fair for democracy when in 1925 all adult males were given the vote. ✅ In the same year a public-security statute was enacted permitting the prosecution of movements held to threaten the imperial order or private property; ⚠️ the two measures should always be cited together. ✅ Hopes were soon dashed: at the beginning of the 1930s the army assumed control of the government.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the pairing of a mass franchise with a security law in the same year is the most compact evidence available about the Japanese system, and it deserves a sentence. ⚠️ **Participation was widened and dissent was criminalised simultaneously**, which tells you that the political class regarded democratisation as a risk to be managed rather than as a principle to be established. ⚠️ It is also the institutional background to the fact recorded in Lesson 2 - that when farmers and industrial workers tried to organise a political party they were ruthlessly suppressed by the police.

### 4. The elite institutions, and why they mattered more than elections

✅ **Fact:** at first the elite groups in society were content to give the government free rein, but after the First World War they began to be more critical. ✅ Especially troublesome were the army and the conservatives, who were strongly entrenched in the House of Peers and in the Privy Council, and who seized every opportunity to discredit the government. ✅ They criticised Baron Shidehara Kijuro, foreign minister from 1924 to 1927, for his conciliatory approach to China, which he believed was the best way to strengthen Japan's economic hold over that country; ✅ the army was itching to interfere in China, which was torn by civil war, and considered his policy soft; ✅ they were strong enough to bring the government down in 1927 and reverse it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this episode is four years before Manchuria and it is the better illustration of the constitutional problem, because it involves no violence at all. ⚠️ A foreign minister with a considered strategy was removed, and his policy reversed, by institutional actors who had not been elected and could not be dismissed. ⚠️ **The army did not need to break the constitution in 1927; it used it.** ⚠️ Anyone who grasps that will understand why the events of 1931 and 1932, when they come, do not require a new theory of a fascist seizure - only an escalation of a capacity that already existed.

### 5. Comparison: three constitutional starting positions in 1929

| Feature | ✅ Italy | ✅ Germany | ✅ Japan |
|---|---|---|---|
| Head of state | King Victor Emmanuel III, who retained the power that let him dismiss Mussolini in 1943 | President Hindenburg, able to dismiss a Chancellor until his death in August 1934 | Emperor, sacred and inviolable, able to dissolve the Diet at will |
| Who commands the armed forces | The crown, in a state where the regime had legalised its own militia | The republic, in a state where the officer corps tended to be anti-republican | ✅ The emperor, directly - not the cabinet and not the Diet |
| An unelected upper tier | The monarchy and the Senate | The presidency | ✅ The House of Peers and the Privy Council, where the army and conservatives were strongly entrenched |
| The route to authoritarian rule | ⚠️ A mass movement admitted by the crown | ⚠️ A mass movement admitted by the president | ⚠️ No movement required; an existing armed institution escaping control |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last row is the comparative finding and should be quoted almost verbatim in any answer that includes Japan. ⚠️ Two of the three cases required an **entry**; the third required only a **failure of restraint**. That difference is precisely why the core account withholds the fascist label from Japan while granting that repression, one-party control and secret policing were present.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** this institutional explanation is too tidy. Constitutions do not act. The Meiji design had existed since 1889 and had produced four decades of civilian-led government, including parliamentary cabinets and an expanding franchise; if design alone were decisive, the collapse should have come much earlier.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is correct and identifies exactly what Lesson 13 must supply. ⚠️ The design is a **standing capacity**, not a cause: it made military autonomy available without making its exercise necessary. ⚠️ What converts capacity into action is a set of dated pressures - the end of the trade boom in mid-1921, the corruption of the Diet, the collapse of raw-silk prices after 1929, rural poverty among the peasantry from whom most army recruits came, and a specific opportunity in Manchuria. ⚠️ The disciplined two-part formulation, which is the shape of the whole Japanese answer: **the constitution supplied the possibility, and the depression supplied the occasion.**

### 7. UPSC application

⚠️ Japan is where comparative answers most often become vague, because candidates have chronology without structure. This lesson supplies the structure.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the constitutional and institutional roots of Japanese militarism.
> ⚠️ "Japanese expansionism in the 1930s was a continuation of Meiji policy by other means." Comment.
> ⚠️ Compare the political systems of Japan and Germany on the eve of the Depression.

**Answer-use guidance.** In any framing that includes Japan alongside the European cases, spend one sentence on the constitutional asymmetry before any narrative: the emperor commanded the armed forces, the cabinet was appointed rather than chosen by the Diet, and the army and conservatives were entrenched in the appointed Peers and the Privy Council. ⚠️ That sentence pre-empts the flattening comparison and makes everything that follows sharper.

### Traps

- Beginning the Japanese story in 1931. ✅ The army destroyed the Shidehara China policy and brought down a government in 1927, without violence.
- Saying Japan had no democracy. ✅ There was an elected Diet from 1890, it could initiate laws, and all adult males received the vote in 1925.
- Saying Japan was a fully democratic parliamentary state before 1931. ✅ The system was far from democratic; the emperor could dissolve the Diet at will, decided war and peace and commanded the armed forces.
- Forgetting the security legislation of 1925 that accompanied the franchise.
- Treating Japanese expansion as a 1930s novelty. ✅ Korea and Manchuria were colonised before the wars of 1894-95 and 1904-5, and German possessions in China were seized in 1914.
- Assuming a fascist party existed. There was no mass rebirth movement, which is the account's stated reason for refusing the label.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1853: Perry at Yokohama with four battleships; treaties within five years, regarded as a national humiliation.
2. ✅ 1868: Meiji Restoration; railways, roads, cotton and silk industry; a parliamentary system modelled on Germany's constitution; unification and centralisation for the first time in over two and a half centuries.
3. ✅ Strategy: occupy neighbouring territories to avoid China's fate; Korea and then Manchuria; wars with China 1894-95 and Russia 1904-5, both won.
4. ✅ 1902 alliance with Britain; 1914 entry on the British side; seizure of German colonies and bases in China.
5. ✅ 1919: represented at Versailles, League member, one of the "Big Five", with a powerful navy, a strong army and great influence in China.
6. ✅ The 1889 constitution: appointed House of Peers, cabinet appointed by the emperor, Privy Council to interpret and safeguard the constitution, elected Diet from 1890.
7. ✅ The emperor: could dissolve the Diet at will, decided war and peace, was commander-in-chief, and was sacred and inviolable.
8. ✅ The Diet's one advantage: it could initiate new laws, so the cabinet was not as susceptible to its own will as expected.
9. ✅ 1925: all adult males given the vote; ✅ accompanied by a public-security statute against movements threatening the imperial order or private property.
10. ✅ Elite hostility after 1918 from the army and conservatives entrenched in the Peers and the Privy Council.
11. ✅ 1927: Shidehara's conciliatory China policy attacked as soft; the government brought down and the policy reversed.
12. ⚠️ The comparative finding: Italy and Germany required a movement to be admitted; Japan required only a failure of restraint over an institution already inside the state.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Japanese case cannot be analysed using the same causal model as the Italian and German cases, and state the model that does fit.

**Model answer:** Because the decisive actor was already inside the constitution. In Italy and Germany an organisation was built outside the state - the *fasci di combattimento* from 1919, the Nazi Party through the 1920s - acquired mass support, and was then admitted to office by a head of state: the king in October 1922 and Hindenburg in January 1933. Every element of the European model presupposes that entry: the mass movement, the leader cult, the electoral breakthrough, the elite bargain. None of those is needed in Japan. Under the constitution formally accepted in 1889 the emperor was commander-in-chief of the armed forces, decided questions of war and peace, could dissolve the Diet whenever he wished and was sacred and inviolable; the cabinet was appointed by the emperor rather than chosen by the Diet; and the army and conservatives were strongly entrenched in the appointed House of Peers and in the Privy Council. The army therefore already possessed a constitutional position that no election could alter and no cabinet could discipline, and it demonstrated this in 1927 without any violence at all, when it attacked Shidehara's conciliatory China policy as soft, brought the government down and had the policy reversed. The model that fits is not admission but escape: a standing institution with reserved constitutional powers progressively escaping civilian restraint under economic and political pressure. That is also why the expansion of the 1930s is better read as a change in who controlled foreign policy than as a change in its direction, since the colonisation of Korea and Manchuria and the wars of 1894-95 and 1904-5 long preceded the crisis.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because the outcome in all three states was authoritarian and expansionist, the process must have been the same. Outcomes converge; mechanisms do not. The examination value of the Japanese case lies precisely in being the control that shows which features of the European cases were necessary and which were merely typical.

*The capacity has now been described. The next lesson supplies the pressures that activated it, the sequence by which party government ended, and the argument about what to call the result.*

## Lesson 13 - Japan 1921-37: trade collapse, assassination, army dominance, and the label debate

### Visual: the pressure chain, the two decisive acts, and the label test

```text
   PRESSURE CHAIN
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   the great trading boom of the war years lasts ONLY TO MID-1921
        -> Europe revives and recovers lost markets
        -> unemployment and industrial unrest in Japan
        -> farmers hit by rapidly falling rice prices after bumper
           harvests
        -> farmers and industrial workers try to form a political
           party -> RUTHLESSLY SUPPRESSED BY THE POLICE
        -> so WORKERS TOO turn against a parliament that poses as
           democratic, allows the left to be suppressed, and takes
           bribes from big business
                              +
   the world economic crisis from 1929
        -> exports shrink disastrously; other countries raise tariffs
        -> RAW SILK, sold mostly to the USA, collapses: by 1932 the
           price is LESS THAN ONE-FIFTH of the 1923 figure
        -> about HALF of Japanese farmers relied on raw silk as well
           as rice -> desperate poverty, especially in the north
        -> factory workers and peasants blame the government and
           big business
        -> *** MOST ARMY RECRUITS WERE PEASANTS ***
           so rank-and-file AND officers were disgusted with what
           they took to be weak parliamentary government
        -> AS EARLY AS 1927 many officers, attracted by fascism,
           were planning to seize power and install a strong
           nationalist government
   ------------------------------------------------------------------

   THE TWO DECISIVE ACTS
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   SEPT 1931  Manchuria - a Chinese province of 30 MILLION people in
              which Japan had valuable investments and trade; the
              Chinese were trying to squeeze out Japanese trade and
              business, a severe blow to an economy already hard hit
              -> JAPANESE ARMY UNITS INVADED AND OCCUPIED MANCHURIA
                 *** WITHOUT PERMISSION FROM THE GOVERNMENT ***
   MAY 1932   PRIME MINISTER INUKAI criticises extremism and is
              ASSASSINATED BY NAVAL OFFICERS, WITH ARMY CADETS
              -> "not surprisingly, his successor felt he had to
                 support the army's actions"
   ------------------------------------------------------------------
   RESULT: FOR THE NEXT 13 YEARS THE ARMY MORE OR LESS RAN THE
   COUNTRY, using methods "similar to those adopted in Italy and
   Germany": ruthless suppression of communists; assassination of
   opponents; tight control of education; a build-up of armaments;
   an aggressive foreign policy aimed at capturing Asian territory
   to serve as markets -> attack on China 1937 -> the Pacific war
   ------------------------------------------------------------------

   THE LABEL TEST APPLIED
   +----------------------------------+-----------------------------+
   | PRESENT IN JAPAN                 | ABSENT IN JAPAN             |
   | repression and secret policing   | a mass movement mobilising  |
   | assassination of opponents       |   for national rebirth      |
   | control of education             | a single charismatic party  |
   | armaments build-up               |   leader displacing the     |
   | aggressive expansion             |   existing head of state    |
   | one-party tendency               | a new constitution or a     |
   |                                  |   party-state replacing the |
   |                                  |   old institutions          |
   +----------------------------------+-----------------------------+
   RECORDED VERDICT: "not, strictly speaking, fascist states: they
   lacked the vital element of mass mobilization in pursuit of the
   rebirth of the nation"
```

*⚠️ The visual separates pressures from acts deliberately. The pressures explain why the army had support; the two acts explain how control changed hands. Conflating them produces the common script in which economic distress somehow "leads to" militarism without anybody doing anything.*

### 1. The rural transmission, which is the distinctive Japanese mechanism

✅ **Fact:** the great trading boom of the war years lasted only until the middle of 1921, when Europe began to revive and recover lost markets. ✅ In Japan, unemployment and industrial unrest developed, and at the same time farmers were hit by the rapidly falling price of rice caused by a series of bumper harvests. ✅ When farmers and industrial workers tried to organise themselves into a political party they were ruthlessly suppressed by the police.

✅ **Fact:** the world economic crisis beginning in 1929 affected Japan severely. ✅ Exports shrank disastrously and other countries introduced or raised tariffs against Japanese goods to protect their own industries. ✅ One of the worst affected trades was the export of raw silk, which went mostly to the United States; after the Wall Street Crash the Americans drastically reduced their imports of raw silk, so that by 1932 the price had fallen to less than one-fifth of the 1923 figure. ✅ This was a further blow for Japanese farmers, since about half of them relied for their livelihood on raw silk as well as rice. ✅ There was desperate poverty, especially in the north, for which factory workers and peasants blamed the government and big business.

✅ **Fact:** and then the sentence that connects the economics to the politics. ✅ Most of the army recruits were peasants; consequently the rank-and-file as well as the officer class were disgusted with what they took to be weak parliamentary government. ✅ As early as 1927, many officers, attracted by fascism, were planning to seize power and introduce a strong nationalist government.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the best-specified transmission mechanism anywhere in the topic, and it should be used whenever a question asks how economic crisis produced political change. ⚠️ In Germany the Depression reached politics through unemployment and the ballot box. ⚠️ In Japan it reached politics through a **single export commodity, a rural social structure and the recruitment base of the armed forces**. A collapse in the American demand for silk impoverished the families from which soldiers came, and the army therefore acquired a grievance of its own that was social rather than professional. ⚠️ That is why the Japanese crisis did not need an electoral channel: the distress reached the institution directly.

✅ **Fact:** note also the date 1927 in that passage - officers planning a seizure four years before Manchuria and two years before the Crash. ⚠️ Whatever the Depression added, it did not create the intention.

### 2. Manchuria: the act that broke civilian control

✅ **Fact:** matters were brought to a head in 1931 by the situation in Manchuria, a large province of China with a population of thirty million, in which Japan had valuable investments and trade. ✅ The Chinese were trying to squeeze out Japanese trade and business, which would have been a severe blow to an economy already hard hit by the depression. ✅ To preserve their economic advantages, Japanese army units staged an incident on the South Manchurian Railway near Mukden on **18 September 1931** and invaded and occupied Manchuria - ✅ without permission from the government.

⚠️ **Boundary note.** ⚠️ What the League of Nations did about Manchuria, what its report found, and what the episode proved about collective security belong to the international-relations owner and are not taught here. ⚠️ What belongs here is the domestic constitutional fact: an army acted abroad without the authority of its own cabinet and was not punished.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the six words "without permission from the government" are the most important in the Japanese section, and an answer should quote the substance of them. ⚠️ They record a **completed constitutional breakdown before any assassination and before any change of cabinet**. Once a government has failed to discipline armed forces that have committed it to a foreign occupation, the location of sovereignty has changed regardless of what the formal documents say. ⚠️ Compare the German equivalent: in 1934 Hitler had to purge his own paramilitary force to satisfy the army. In Japan the army required nobody's satisfaction.

### 3. Assassination, and the end of party cabinets

✅ **Fact:** when Prime Minister Inukai criticised extremism he was assassinated in May 1932 by **naval officers, with army cadets** taking part; ✅ not surprisingly, his successor felt he had to support the army's actions. ✅ For the next thirteen years the army more or less ran the country, introducing similar methods to those adopted in Italy and Germany: ruthless suppression of communists, assassination of opponents, tight control of education, a build-up of armaments, and an aggressive foreign policy which aimed to capture territory in Asia to serve as markets for Japanese exports. ✅ This led to an attack on China in 1937 - opening with the clash at the Marco Polo Bridge outside Beijing on 7 July - and to participation in the Second World War in the Pacific.

⚠️ **One correction to the core account, stated once and not repeated.** ⚠️ The printed account describes the killers as "a group of army officers". ✅ The verified identification is that the assassins were young **naval** officers, acting with army cadets. ⚠️ The correction matters analytically as well as factually: it shows that insubordination in 1932 was not confined to one service, which is why the phrase "the army took over" understates what had happened to civilian control. ⚠️ Everywhere else in this session the assassination is attributed as naval officers with army cadets.

✅ Two further features of the 1930s belong in a complete account. ✅ Political violence against ministers was not confined to 1932: a party prime minister was shot in November 1930 amid nationalist fury at a naval limitation agreement, dying of his injuries the following year. ✅ And the army was not a single actor: a radical faction seeking direct imperial rule and an immediate purge of the political order attempted a coup in February 1936, assassinating several government and military figures; it was suppressed, its leaders were purged, and the more methodical faction that favoured working through existing institutions and a planned military build-up emerged dominant.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that factional detail changes the shape of the answer, and it is the point at which a good script separates from an average one. ⚠️ The suppression of the February 1936 rising was not a defeat for military influence; it was a **consolidation** of it. ⚠️ The radicals were eliminated and the winners were the officers who preferred to run the state through its existing ministries rather than to smash them - which is the deepest reason why Japan never acquired a party-state, a new constitution or a leader cult of the European type. ⚠️ **The faction that won was the one with no interest in building a fascist movement.**

### 4. The emperor, and the limits of the record

✅ **Fact:** some historians blame Emperor Hirohito who, though he deplored the attack on Manchuria, refused to become involved in political controversy, afraid to risk his orders for a withdrawal being ignored. ✅ The historian Richard Storry claims that it would have been better for Japan and for the world if the risk had been taken, and believes Hirohito's prestige was so great that the majority of officers would have obeyed him if he had tried to restrain the attacks on Manchuria and China. ✅ When the Second World War began, it seems the emperor genuinely wanted to stay out of it and hesitated over whether to sign an alliance with Nazi Germany; ✅ however, after the early successes of the German campaigns he agreed to the alliance and eventually to the attack on Pearl Harbor, giving the military the chance to achieve their ambition of continuing the conquest of China and south-east Asia.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the emperor's position is the sharpest test of the comparison, and it is worth stating carefully. ⚠️ Mussolini and Hitler were the sources of authority in their systems - one as head of government under a king he could ignore, the other after August 1934 as head of state, government and armed forces with a personal oath sworn to him. ⚠️ Hirohito was the source of authority in a system he did not direct. ⚠️ The Japanese regime thus had the **form** of sacral leadership without a leader who governed, which is close to the opposite of the European arrangement, where men who governed manufactured a sacral form for themselves. ⚠️ Storry's judgement should be reported as a judgement, since it rests on a counterfactual about whether orders would have been obeyed.

⚠️ **Ideology note, with attribution.** ✅ The regime's legitimating doctrine was an imperial and national-essence ideology propagated through the school system rather than a party programme, and the standard official text of that doctrine was published in 1937. ⚠️ The comparison to hold is that the content was **restorative and hierarchical** rather than palingenetic in the European sense: it sanctified an existing order and an existing dynasty instead of promising the birth of a new nation out of decadence.

### 5. So was it fascism? The debate, stated fairly

| Position | Case for | Case against |
|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ **Japan was fascist** | ✅ The account itself says the army introduced methods similar to those adopted in Italy and Germany; ✅ officers attracted by fascism were planning a seizure as early as 1927; repression, controlled education, rearmament and expansion are all present | ✅ The same account states that Japan was not strictly a fascist state |
| ⚠️ **Japan was a military dictatorship** | ✅ The army more or less ran the country for thirteen years; the decisive acts were an unauthorised occupation and an assassination by officers; the winning faction preferred to rule through existing ministries | ⚠️ Risks understating the ideological and mass-educational dimension |
| ⚠️ **"Fascism from above"** | ✅ Maruyama Masao's influential formulation: a fascism imposed by military, bureaucratic and court elites rather than built by a movement from below | ⚠️ Stretches the term by removing the feature the core account treats as definitive |
| ⚠️ **The label obscures more than it reveals** | ⚠️ Gregory Kasza's caution: no mass fascist party, no single charismatic dictator, many features of authoritarianism and militarism but not the defining traits of European fascism | ⚠️ Leaves the resemblances unexplained and needs a positive account in their place |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the position to defend in an examination is the second, stated with the third and fourth acknowledged. ⚠️ **Japan produced a militarist authoritarian regime that borrowed fascist methods without acquiring fascist form.** ⚠️ The methods travelled - controlled education, suppression of the left, armaments, expansion - because they are the standard toolkit of any modern authoritarian state. ⚠️ The form did not travel, because there was no mass movement, no party-state, no new constitution and no leader displacing the sovereign. ⚠️ Stating it that way explains the resemblance instead of merely denying the label, which is what separates the top band from the middle.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** refusing the fascist label for Japan is Eurocentric. It defines fascism by the specific institutional forms two European states happened to adopt, and then finds that a non-European case fails to match them - which guarantees the conclusion in advance.

⚠️ **Reply:** this is the strongest objection in the lesson and should be met directly rather than brushed aside. ⚠️ Three points answer it. First, the criterion is not geographical but functional: the test is whether a mass movement mobilised a population for national rebirth, and that test is applied in this session to European cases too - Primo de Rivera's Spain fails it, and so does Salazar's Portugal. ⚠️ Second, the account applies it in both directions across continents: it rejects the label for Peron and Vargas in Latin America while recording that they borrowed fascist ideas, and it quotes Hobsbawm's observation that they created labour movements where European fascism destroyed them. ⚠️ Third, refusing the label is not a claim that the Japanese regime was milder; it was expansionist, repressive and ruled by assassination and occupation. ⚠️ **Classification and condemnation are different operations**, and an answer that keeps them apart is doing history rather than rhetoric.

### 7. UPSC application

⚠️ This lesson is the one that makes a three-country comparative question answerable rather than approximate.

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Japan between the wars was fascist in practice if not in name." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Examine the reasons for the rise of militarism in Japan in the 1930s.
> ⚠️ Compare the collapse of parliamentary government in Japan and Germany.

**Answer-use guidance.** For the first framing, state the verdict early and spend the body proving both halves: what travelled and what did not. ⚠️ Use the silk price, the peasant recruitment base and the unauthorised occupation as the three pieces of evidence that no competitor script will have, and close with the classification-versus-condemnation distinction.

### Traps

- Saying the Japanese government ordered the Manchurian occupation. ✅ Army units acted without permission from the government.
- Dating military intentions to the Depression. ✅ Officers were planning a seizure as early as 1927.
- Saying Japan had a fascist party in power. ✅ There was no mass rebirth movement; the account's refusal of the label rests on precisely that absence.
- Treating the February 1936 rising as the high point of military power. ⚠️ Its suppression consolidated the influence of the faction that preferred to work through existing institutions.
- Saying the emperor directed the aggression. ✅ The account records that he deplored the attack on Manchuria and feared his orders would be ignored, and reports Storry's contrary judgement as a judgement.
- Using Manchuria to discuss the League of Nations. That material belongs to the international-relations owner.
- Forgetting the rural transmission. ✅ Raw silk below one-fifth of its 1923 price by 1932, about half of farmers dependent on it, and most army recruits drawn from the peasantry.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The trade boom ended in mid-1921; unemployment, industrial unrest and falling rice prices followed; worker and farmer parties were ruthlessly suppressed by the police.
2. ✅ After 1929 exports shrank disastrously and tariffs rose abroad; raw silk, sold mostly to the United States, fell below one-fifth of its 1923 price by 1932.
3. ✅ About half of Japanese farmers depended on raw silk as well as rice; desperate poverty, especially in the north.
4. ✅ Most army recruits were peasants, so rank-and-file and officers alike were disgusted with weak parliamentary government.
5. ✅ As early as 1927, many officers attracted by fascism were planning to seize power.
6. ✅ September 1931: army units invaded and occupied Manchuria - a province of thirty million in which Japan had valuable investments - without permission from the government.
7. ✅ May 1932: Prime Minister Inukai criticised extremism and was assassinated by naval officers, with army cadets taking part; his successor felt he had to support the army.
8. ✅ For thirteen years the army more or less ran the country, with methods similar to those of Italy and Germany: suppression of communists, assassination of opponents, controlled education, armaments and expansion aimed at Asian markets.
9. ✅ A party prime minister was shot in November 1930 over a naval limitation agreement; ✅ a radical faction's coup attempt in February 1936 was suppressed, ⚠️ consolidating the faction that preferred to rule through existing ministries.
10. ✅ Hirohito deplored Manchuria but feared his orders would be ignored; Storry judges that the majority of officers would have obeyed him.
11. ✅ 1937: full-scale war in China from the Marco Polo Bridge clash of 7 July; then participation in the Pacific war.
12. ⚠️ Verdict: a militarist authoritarian regime that borrowed fascist methods without acquiring fascist form - no mass movement, no party-state, no new constitution, no leader displacing the sovereign.

### Concept check

**Question:** Identify the single most important evidential difference between the German and Japanese routes to authoritarian government, and explain what follows from it for a comparative answer.

**Model answer:** The difference is that in Germany the change of regime required an act by the head of state, while in Japan it required only that an existing armed institution act without authority and go unpunished. Hitler was appointed Chancellor by Hindenburg in January 1933 after the Nazis had lost thirty-four seats in November 1932, because Papen and Schleicher believed they could control him; he then needed the March election, the Enabling Law of 23 March 1933, forcible coordination and finally the purge of his own paramilitary wing in June 1934 to obtain the army's oath after Hindenburg's death. Every stage presupposes a movement outside the state seeking entry. In Japan the sequence is the opposite. Under the constitution of 1889 the emperor commanded the armed forces, the cabinet was appointed rather than chosen by the Diet, and the army and conservatives were entrenched in the appointed Peers and the Privy Council; the army used that position to destroy Shidehara's China policy in 1927 without violence, and in September 1931 army units invaded and occupied Manchuria without permission from the government. When Prime Minister Inukai criticised extremism he was assassinated in May 1932 by naval officers, with army cadets taking part, and his successor felt obliged to support the army; for thirteen years the army more or less ran the country. What follows for a comparative answer is that the two cases require different explanatory variables. Germany requires a mass movement, an electoral crisis and an elite miscalculation. Japan requires a constitutional design, a rural economic shock transmitted through the army's peasant recruitment base, and a failure to discipline insubordination. It also follows that the core account's refusal of the fascist label for Japan is not a quibble: what is missing there is exactly the element that does all the explanatory work in the European cases.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that shared methods prove a shared nature. Suppression of communists, controlled education, rearmament and expansion are the common equipment of modern authoritarian states and tell you little about type. The classification turns on whether a mass movement mobilised a population for national rebirth, and on that test Japan is a militarist authoritarian regime rather than a fascist one - which is a statement about form, not about severity.

*All three cases are now complete. The remaining three lessons stop narrating and start comparing: first what these regimes did to the societies they governed, then why the societies did not remove them, and finally how the whole thing becomes an answer.*

## Lesson 14 - Mass politics compared: mobilisation, propaganda, corporatism, gender, youth, business and church
