## What a topic with one owned question requires

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** one owned question is a small bank and a large signal. ⚠️ The signal is that when this topic is set, it is set as an **evaluative systems question** rather than as a narrative one - which tells a candidate what to rehearse.

1. ⚠️ **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** The five probable framing families - definition, conditions, comparison, regime performance, and society - each have a spine in Lesson 16. Practise producing the spine from a cold stem in ninety seconds.
2. ⚠️ **Rehearse the controls.** Fourteen factual-risk items, of which the March on Rome, the Nazi vote share and the date of the Enabling Law are the most frequently breached.
3. ⚠️ **Rehearse Japan separately.** It is the item most likely to be thin under time pressure and the item that most distinguishes a script, because it requires a definitional operation rather than recall.
4. ⚠️ **Rehearse the qualification.** Every directive in this family rewards a closing qualification, and four are supplied in Lesson 16 section 4 of the decision procedure.
5. ⚠️ **Use the recorded probable framings as the practice bank.** The two canonical owners record seven between them: the conditions that enabled fascism and Nazism; how far Mussolini's Italy was totalitarian; a comparison of Italian fascism, German Nazism and Japanese militarism; whether Hitler's dictatorship rested more on consent than coercion; the failures of parliamentary politics; how far Japan and Spain were fascist; and the challenge to the democratic state system. ⚠️ The six original questions below are built to cover all seven.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - definition and the common crisis, Lessons 1 to 2

### Cumulative check A1 - the test, applied to four cases at once

**Question:** Apply the mass-mobilisation test to Italy, Germany, Japan and Spain, and state in each case what the classification licenses you to say and what it forbids.

**Model answer:** The test asks whether a mass movement mobilised a population in pursuit of national rebirth. Italy passes: the *fasci di combattimento* were founded in 1919, organised squads from late 1920, contested elections, and after 1922 built a party-state with a leader cult, youth organisations covering ages six to twenty-one and a leisure apparatus in *Dopolavoro*. The classification licenses calling Italy the original fascist state; it forbids calling it totalitarian, because the king remained head of state and dismissed Mussolini in 1943, the Church retained an independence secured by the Lateran Treaty of 1929, and critics such as Benedetto Croce survived. Germany passes and radicalises: a mass party, a leader cult, forcible coordination, a rebirth doctrine expressed as the *Volksgemeinschaft*, and race as the fourth founding principle. The classification licenses treating Germany as the extreme member of the family; it forbids treating it as merely a more severe Italy, because race in Germany governed citizenship, marriage and reproduction from 1935 whereas in Italy comparable legislation arrived with the race charter of July 1938 and the decrees of that autumn, as a tactical consequence of the Axis. Japan fails: there was no mass rebirth movement, the army acted as an institution when units occupied Manchuria in September 1931 without government permission, Inukai was assassinated by naval officers in May 1932, and the constitution of 1889 and the emperor were retained throughout. The classification licenses calling Japan a militarist authoritarian regime that borrowed fascist methods; it forbids calling it fascist without qualification, and it forbids inferring mildness from the refusal of the label. Spain fails twice over: Primo de Rivera was a military dictator and not a fascist, and Franco's post-1939 order restored the Church as the established state Church, required civil servants to be Catholic and cancelled the Republic's legislation on women - a clerical-conservative restoration rather than a mobilising party-state.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the test as a hierarchy of wickedness. It sorts by political form, not by severity. Japan's exclusion from the category coexists with assassination of ministers, suppression of the left, controlled education and a war of conquest in China from 1937.

### Cumulative check A2 - necessary conditions against sufficient ones

**Question:** Seven mechanisms of democratic failure operated in Italy, Germany and Japan. Explain why listing them does not answer the 2021 question, and state what must be added.

**Model answer:** Because the seven are necessary conditions of the outcome and not sufficient ones, and the question asks for an evaluation rather than an inventory. Unmet war expectation, monetary and income destruction, coalition immobilism, armed politics, fear of revolution, elite defection and weak opposition all operated in the three states; but versions of the first four operated in France, Britain and the Netherlands too, which retained parliamentary government. What has to be added is a ranking, and the ranking is settled by asking which mechanism actually transfers power. It is the sixth. In Italy the arithmetic in May 1921 gave the fascists thirty-five seats against 123 socialist seats, and the account says plainly that there should have been no question of a takeover; eighteen months later Mussolini was prime minister, and what changed in between was the alignment of business finance, property-owners, the papacy and finally a king who refused the state of emergency that Facta was prepared to use. In Germany the Nazis lost thirty-four seats and about two million votes in November 1932 and were in the Chancellery ten weeks later, because Papen and Schleicher, with Reichswehr support, persuaded Hindenburg. In Japan the transfer required no movement at all, only Inukai's successor feeling obliged to support army actions that had never been authorised. The seventh mechanism is the permissive condition behind the sixth: the Italian socialists refused Giolitti, and the German communists refused the Social Democrats, so no coalition existed to make defection costly. The evaluation therefore reads: distress made these democracies unpopular; elite defection made them defunct.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that more mechanisms make a stronger answer. Seven unranked causes score lower than three ranked ones, because the directive asks for a judgement about the seriousness and character of the challenge, and a judgement requires a hierarchy.

## Block B - the two European regimes, Lessons 3 to 11

### Cumulative check B1 - the same operation, performed differently

**Question:** Both regimes converted an electoral minority into unchallengeable power. Compare the two conversions and identify what each reveals about the state it operated in.

**Model answer:** The Italian conversion was legislative and slow; the German was administrative and fast. Mussolini entered office in October 1922 as prime minister of a coalition with four fascist ministers of twelve, holding special powers only to the end of 1923. He legalised his squads as the National State Voluntary Militia, then rewrote the electoral rules through the Acerbo Law of November 1923 so that the party with most votes would automatically take two-thirds of the seats, and the April 1924 election returned 404 seats against 107 - a result the account attributes both to a genuine desire for strong government and to violence and fraud. The constitutional conversion followed by ordinary process in 1925 and 1926: responsibility to the king alone, rule by decree, and an electorate cut from about ten million to three million. Consolidation was politically largely complete by 1930, eight years after entry. The German conversion used the state's own machinery within weeks. Goering, as Prussian interior minister, controlled the police in the largest German state, replaced senior officers and deputised 50,000 auxiliaries from the SA and SS; the Reichstag fire of 27 February supplied a pretext for banning the communists and arresting about four thousand of them; the election of 5 March still produced only 288 of 647 seats on forty-four per cent of those voting; and the Enabling Law of 23 March was carried 441 to 94 after the eighty-one communist deputies had been arrested or driven into hiding and some socialists were physically excluded. All constraints were gone by August 1934. What each reveals is the strength of what stood in the way: Italy had a monarchy, a Church and a slow-moving administrative state that the regime had to negotiate with over years, while Germany had a centralised bureaucratic apparatus and a federal police structure that could be captured at a stroke and turned on its own citizens.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the difference in speed as a difference in ruthlessness alone. The German conversion was faster partly because the instruments available were better - a modern police bureaucracy, a large state within the federation and an emergency power in the constitution - and partly because there was no monarch and no concordat-protected Church already occupying part of the political space.

### Cumulative check B2 - the economic records, judged by the same criteria

**Question:** Judge the Italian and German economic records against three criteria - employment, structural change and distribution - and state what the comparison contributes to a question about why each regime lasted.

**Model answer:** On employment the records diverge sharply. Italy never eliminated unemployment; it rose to 1.1 million in the Depression, the lira was not devalued until 1936, and real wages fell by over ten per cent while strikes were illegal. Germany reduced unemployment from over six million in January 1933 to just over two million by the end of 1935 and to negligible levels by 1939, which the account calls probably the most important reason for Hitler's popularity - though the routes included conscription from 1935, rearmament from 1934 and the vacancies created by purging Jews and anti-Nazis from the professions. On structural change both failed in the same direction. Italy by 1940 had not become self-sufficient in coal, oil or steel, produced less than Belgium, retained the dualist division between an industrial north and a backward south, and had become economically dependent on Germany; only about a tenth of its flagship land-reclamation programme had been carried out by 1939. Germany never achieved autarky either, and the account's own argument is that it could not have done so without seizing territory in eastern Europe, while the military budget absorbed fifty-two per cent of government spending in 1938-9 against a huge deficit. On distribution both regimes transferred from labour to capital. Italian farm labourers' wages fell twenty to forty per cent in the 1930s and there was no welfare state or health insurance until 1943; in Germany, Evans records no marked improvement for most middle-class wage-earners and declining per-capita consumption of basic foodstuffs, with wage gains only through longer hours, while industrialists were freed of unions and repurchased shares cheaply. The contribution to the durability question is this: Germany delivered security where Italy delivered only spectacle, which is why the German regime obtained a broader acquiescence, and why Italian disenchantment had probably set in long before the war while the German image survived until 1941.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the German record was therefore the better one. It was the more effective one at producing compliance, which is a different claim; and its effectiveness depended on instruments - rearmament, conscription and racial purges - that were not separable from the regime's other purposes.

## Block C - Japan, comparison and judgement, Lessons 12 to 16

### Cumulative check C1 - the control case

**Question:** Explain how the Japanese case functions as a control that tests which features of the European cases were necessary and which were merely typical.

**Model answer:** A control shares the outcome and lacks a suspected cause, so it shows whether the cause was needed. Japan reached an authoritarian, expansionist outcome - suppression of communists, assassination of opponents, controlled education, rearmament and a war in China from 1937 - without a mass movement, without a party-state, without a new constitution and without a leader displacing the sovereign. That immediately removes three features from the list of necessary conditions for interwar authoritarianism: a charismatic party leader, a mass mobilising movement, and the replacement of the existing constitutional order. Each was typical of Italy and Germany and none was required for the outcome. What the case leaves standing as necessary is more interesting. Economic shock is retained but with a different transmission: exports shrank after 1929, raw silk sold mostly to the United States fell below a fifth of its 1923 price by 1932, about half of Japanese farmers depended on it as well as rice, and most army recruits were peasants - so distress reached the decisive institution directly rather than through an electorate. Elite hostility to parliamentary government is retained: the army and conservatives were entrenched in the appointed House of Peers and the Privy Council and brought down the Shidehara China policy in 1927 without violence. Weak or suppressed opposition is retained: worker and farmer parties had been ruthlessly suppressed by the police, so the parliament was distrusted by the left as repressive and by the right as corrupt. And the failure to punish insubordination is retained as the decisive act, since army units occupied Manchuria in September 1931 without permission from the government and nothing followed. The control therefore refines the general theory: interwar authoritarianism required distress, elite disaffection and an unpunished breach of civilian control - and a mass movement only where no armed institution already stood outside that control.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using the control to conclude that ideology did not matter. It mattered greatly in Germany, where race governed the legal definition of membership, and it explains why the German regime radicalised in ways the Japanese one did not. The control shows that a mass movement was not necessary for authoritarian rule, not that doctrine was irrelevant to what authoritarian rule then did.

### Cumulative check C2 - responsibility, stated without collective blame

**Question:** Set out how responsibility for these regimes should be attributed, and demonstrate the method on one contested case.

**Model answer:** The method has four rules. Name institutions and individuals rather than nations: governments, cabinets, ministries, parties, militias, courts, army units and identifiable people. Distinguish those who initiated from those who complied and from those who benefited. Report collective formulations found in the sources as those writers' positions rather than adopting them. And preserve the evidence of variation inside every population, because that evidence is what makes the first three rules factually and not merely politely correct. The contested case is the debate on whether Nazism was a continuation of German history. Shelley Baranowski's position is recorded with specific evidence: difficulties in Germany's pre-1914 African colonies, a Prussian doctrine of complete destruction of enemy forces, mass killing in Tanganyika and Namibia, and the brutal suppression of nationalist movements in former Russian territories in 1918 - and she nonetheless stops short of a general genocidal mentality and states that assembly-line extermination had no precedent. Sir Lewis Namier's position is collective and is reported as his: that attempts to absolve the German people are unconvincing and that the Third Reich arose from the people. Gerhard Ritter and K. D. Bracher argue the opposite, treating Nazism as a grotesque departure from normal development, which the account calls probably the majority view. Ian Kershaw combines them: the mentalities that made the rise possible were products of strands of German political culture recognisable before 1914 and most elements feeding into Nazism were peculiarly German, yet Hitler was neither an inevitable product nor an accident, since without the unique conditions of his rise he would have been nothing. The method then delivers the verdict: preconditions were long-term and national, the outcome was contingent, and the acts were committed by named institutions and people - which is also the only account consistent with the recorded fact that the boycott of 1 April 1933 met public apathy and was cancelled, and with the Munich students executed in 1943.

**Misconception to avoid:** Thinking that refusing collective attribution amounts to exculpation. It does the opposite: it makes the charge specific. Naming the ministries, courts, militias, industrial beneficiaries and denouncers is a heavier indictment than a sentence about a nation, because it can be evidenced.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ These six questions are original and are built to cover the seven probable framings recorded by the two canonical owners. ⚠️ Each model answer is written inside its stated ceiling - 141, 147, 246, 242, 245 and 250 words against limits of 150, 150, 250, 250, 250 and 250 - and follows the same discipline: claim, named evidence, analysis, qualification. ⚠️ The scoring note after each identifies what an examiner would reward in that specific answer and is different every time.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ Explain why the parliamentary systems of Italy and Germany proved incapable of decisive government in the years after 1918. *(Directive: explain.)*

**Model answer.** Both systems combined fair representation with unworkable arithmetic. Italy adopted manhood suffrage and proportional representation for 1919; after May 1921 at least nine parties sat in parliament and five cabinets with shaky majorities came and went by 1922, so no consistent policy was possible. Germany's proportional system produced at least eight Reichstag groups in 1928, the largest holding only 153 seats. Design was compounded by behaviour: German parties had no parliamentary experience, because before 1919 the Chancellor and not the Reichstag held final authority, while communists and nationalists rejected democracy outright and the communists refused to work with the Social Democrats. The same refusal appears in Italy, where the socialists would not combine with Giolitti. Qualification: fragmentation was an arithmetical fault of design, the refusal to combine a political choice made inside it - and the second was the more damaging.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** At this ceiling the examiner wants one structural cause and one behavioural cause, precisely evidenced, not a survey of interwar politics. The answer earns its marks by separating design from choice and by naming the same refusal in both countries, which shows a pattern rather than two anecdotes. Adding a third country would force out the qualification, which is a bad trade at 150 words.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ Bring out the significance of the March on Rome of October 1922 in the fascist accession to power. *(Directive: bring out.)*

**Model answer.** Its significance is double and points in opposite directions. As an event it was slight: about 50,000 blackshirts converged on Rome, Facta was prepared to resist, and many historians believe the regular army could easily have dispersed squads that were disorderly, poorly armed and often travelling by train. The outcome was decided elsewhere. King Victor Emmanuel III refused to declare a state of emergency and invited Mussolini, who had remained in Milan and arrived by rail, while army and police stood aside - an enormous bluff which came off. As myth it was central: the fascists fostered a story of heroic seizure, because a movement that made violence integral to its way of life required a violent origin. Qualification: two years of squad attacks on socialist offices, presses and councillors had already destroyed organised opposition in provincial Italy, so legality describes the final step, not the process.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** The marks here lie in the refusal of both simple readings. A script that says "Mussolini seized power" is factually wrong; one that says "he was simply invited" ignores two years of squad violence. This answer fixes the event, relocates the decision to the crown, and then uses the myth itself as evidence about the regime's conception of legitimacy - which converts a factual correction into an analytical point.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ "The Great Depression made the Nazi accession to power inevitable." Critically examine. *(Directive: critically examine.)*

**Model answer.** The statement is right about the condition and wrong about the modality. The economic case is strong. After the Crash of October 1929 the United States stopped new loans and recalled short-term ones, producing a currency-confidence crisis, a run on the banks, contracting exports and factory closures, with unemployment approaching four million by mid-1931 and passing six million by spring 1932; Bruning's cuts to social services, benefit and official salaries produced no quick results and cost the republic much working-class support. Nazi seats track the cycle in both directions: thirty-two in March 1924, fourteen in December 1924 and twelve in 1928 during prosperity, then 107 in 1930 and 230 of 608 in July 1932. The account records that without the crisis it is doubtful Hitler would have attained power. The inevitability claim fails at November 1932, when the Nazis lost thirty-four seats and about two million votes, Bruning's policies appeared to be working and the republic seemed to be weathering the storm. Ten weeks later Hitler was Chancellor, appointed not by voters but by Hindenburg after Papen and Schleicher argued that he could be controlled, that his 230 seats would supplement the Nationalists' thirty-seven, and that the communists' eighty-nine seats were the real danger. Kershaw's judgement is that a Hitler Chancellorship might have been avoided. Qualification: the republic's own survival may still have been over-determined, since the conservative groups and the army were ready to abandon it - but the identity of its successor was not.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** A "critically examine" directive requires the counter-evidence to occupy real space, and here it is a single decisive fact rather than a list. What lifts the answer is that it separates two propositions the stem runs together - the fall of the republic and the accession of the Nazis - and gives them different verdicts, which is precisely the distinction the evidence base makes and most scripts miss.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ Examine how far the Italian fascist regime succeeded in controlling Italian society. *(Directive: examine.)*

**Model answer.** It controlled institutions thoroughly and people incompletely, and the regime's own actions record the shortfall. The institutional reach was real: elected town councils and mayors were abolished and towns run by officials from Rome alongside local party bosses; strikes and lockouts were banned and twenty-two corporations organised by 1934; teachers wore uniforms and swore loyalty, textbooks were rewritten and children were encouraged to criticise unenthusiastic teachers; press censorship banned hostile papers or replaced their editors; and the electorate was cut from about ten million to three million in 1926. Against that stand three measures of limited penetration. Compulsion in the youth organisations arrived only in 1937, fifteen years in, so membership had until then been voluntary and in competition with Catholic bodies. A Ministry of Popular Culture was created in the same year, which the account reads as evidence that fifteen years of propaganda had been less successful than hoped. And *Dopolavoro*, though genuinely popular, is judged to have failed to arouse enthusiasm for the fascist system. The structural limit was institutional: the Lateran Treaty of 1929 conceded Vatican sovereignty, an official religion and compulsory religious instruction, leaving the Church an alternative focus of loyalty Mussolini could not sideline, while the king remained head of state. Qualification: the account's verdict is that the population tolerated fascism while it appeared to bring benefits and tired of it when wartime failure exposed its inadequacies - compliance conditional on performance, not control of belief.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** The distinguishing move is treating the regime's later measures as evidence against its earlier claims. Any competent script lists the instruments of control; this one uses the dates of the instruments - 1937 for compulsion and for the ministry - as a measurement of prior failure, and then closes with the structural limit rather than with an impression.

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ Compare the rise of authoritarian regimes in Italy, Germany and Japan, bringing out both the common conditions and the differences of mechanism. *(Directive: compare.)*

**Model answer.** Fascism proper requires a mass movement mobilising for national rebirth; Italy and Germany qualify, Japan does not, so the comparison must be graded. Four conditions were common. Unmet expectation: Italy received only four promised territories and was cheated over Fiume; the German republic carried the Versailles stigma and the stab-in-the-back legend. Income destruction: the lira fell from 5 to 28 to the dollar by 1921, the mark reached twenty-one billion to the pound by November 1923, and Japanese raw silk fell below a fifth of its 1923 price by 1932. Discredited parliaments: nine Italian parties and five cabinets, eight German groups in 1928, and a Japanese Diet distrusted by right and left alike. Armed politics: blackshirt squads, the *Freikorps*, and ministers killed by serving officers in Japan. The mechanisms then diverge. Italy's movement was admitted by a king who refused a state of emergency; Germany's by a president persuaded by Papen and Schleicher after the party lost thirty-four seats; Japan required no admission, because army units occupied Manchuria in September 1931 without government permission and naval officers assassinated Inukai in May 1932, after which the army ran the country for thirteen years inside an unaltered constitution. Two differences are of kind: race was Germany's fourth founding principle while Italy's racial laws of autumn 1938 followed the Axis, and Italy and Japan kept heads of state who were never displaced - which is why a king dismissed Mussolini in 1943 and nobody could dismiss Hitler.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** At twenty marks the examiner expects the comparison to be organised by variable rather than by country, and expects the differences to be ranked. This answer opens with the definitional test so that Japan becomes the analytical engine rather than a third narrative, and it closes on two differences of kind, each with a dated instance. Note that no casualty figure and no severity ranking appears; the discrimination is structural throughout.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ "These regimes survived through consent rather than coercion." Critically evaluate with reference to interwar Italy and Germany. *(Directive: critically evaluate.)*

**Model answer.** The proposition is half right, unmeasurable as stated, and best replaced by a periodised account of conditional compliance. The consent evidence is substantial. The core account states it would be wrong to suggest Hitler held power simply by terrorising the nation, and that apolitical people could usually live quite happily; unemployment fell from over six million to just over two million by end-1935 and to negligible levels by 1939; Strength through Joy supplied holidays and tickets; Aly records laws extending social security, doubling paid holidays and restricting eviction, and argues the dictatorship rested on a mutual calculation of interest. Kershaw's *Hitler Myth* shows the leader seen as above daily politics, with propertied groups approving of camps described as re-education centres. The coercion evidence is equally firm: partial courts, camps from 1933, six Munich students executed in February 1943, and Italy's exile or murder of opponents including Matteotti and Amendola. Both are limited by one fact - the Gestapo had forty-three officials for Essen's 650,000 people and depended on volunteered denunciations. Evans supplies the missing term: boredom, escapism and survival strategies, with people retreating into private, family and church life where no channel of criticism existed. Qualification, and the decisive one: consent cannot be measured where the institutions expressing dissent have been abolished, so the defensible claim concerns compliance - which held while these regimes delivered security and broke when they demanded sacrifice, in Italy over the war and the 1938 dismissals and in Germany as the image faded during 1941.

⚠️ **Scoring note.** The mark here turns on the epistemic move in the qualification. A script that picks a side has answered a question the evidence cannot settle; this one explains why it cannot be settled, substitutes a claim that can be evidenced, and then dates the failure of compliance in both countries. Three historians are used and each does a different job, which is the correct density at this ceiling.

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ These are the errors that recur most often in scripts on this topic. ⚠️ Each entry names the error, explains why it is wrong, and gives the corrected formulation in a form that can be transferred straight into an answer.

## Error family A - the accessions

**A1. "Mussolini seized power in the March on Rome."**
⚠️ Wrong: Facta was prepared to resist, the king refused a state of emergency and invited him, he arrived by train from Milan, and the account calls the march an enormous bluff which came off.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Power was transferred, not seized: the king declined the state of emergency his prime minister was ready to use, and invited Mussolini to form a government."

**A2. "Hitler was elected Chancellor."**
⚠️ Wrong twice: the office was never elective, and the Nazis had just lost thirty-four seats and about two million votes in November 1932.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Hitler was appointed Chancellor in January 1933 by President Hindenburg, at the urging of Papen and Schleicher, after the Nazi vote had begun to fall."

**A3. "The Nazis won a majority in March 1933."**
⚠️ Wrong: 288 of 647 seats on almost ninety per cent turnout, thirty-six short of the 324 needed, with forty-four per cent of those voting.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Even after fighting the campaign with 50,000 deputised party auxiliaries and after banning the communists, the Nazis took 288 of 647 seats and never won an overall majority in a free election."

**A4. "The Japanese government ordered the occupation of Manchuria."**
⚠️ Wrong, and the error destroys the analytical point: army units acted without permission from the government.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Japanese army units invaded and occupied Manchuria in September 1931 without the government's permission, and no discipline followed - which is where effective civilian control ended."

## Error family B - the regimes

**B1. "Mussolini created a totalitarian state."**
⚠️ Wrong by the account's own verdict: he did not succeed in creating a completely totalitarian system, the king remained head of state and dismissed him in 1943, the Church remained an alternative focus of loyalty, and critics such as Croce survived.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Fascist Italy was authoritarian with totalitarian ambitions rather than a realised totalitarian state."

**B2. "The corporate state was fascism's economic doctrine."**
⚠️ Wrong as a description of what operated: even sympathisers admitted it failed to control production or end class conflict, industrialists bought their freedom through party subscriptions, and the account calls it little more than a propaganda exercise and a way of controlling workers.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The corporate state removed workers' bargaining institutions and left employers considerable freedom; twenty-two corporations existed by 1934 and settled nothing that mattered."

**B3. "Nazism was German fascism."**
⚠️ Wrong as an equation: the account lists six differences, of which the position of race is decisive - a founding principle in Germany, and in Italy a tactical adoption with the race charter of July 1938 and the decrees of that autumn, after the Axis and the Anti-Comintern Pact.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Nazism belongs to the fascist family and differs in kind on one axis: biological race organised citizenship, marriage and reproduction in Germany, whereas Italian racial legislation was derivative and late."

**B4. "Japan became fascist in the 1930s."**
⚠️ Wrong without qualification: the account states that Japan, Spain and Portugal were not strictly fascist states because they lacked mass mobilisation for national rebirth.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Japan produced a militarist authoritarian regime that borrowed fascist methods - suppression of the left, controlled education, rearmament, expansion - without acquiring fascist form."

## Error family C - causation

**C1. "The Depression caused fascism."**
⚠️ Wrong as a general statement: Mussolini took office in October 1922, seven years before the Crash.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The Depression was decisive in Germany, where the account records that without it Hitler would probably not have attained power, and it was the trigger in Japan through the collapse of raw-silk prices; in Italy it worsened a regime that already existed."

**C2. "Big business put these men in power."**
⚠️ Wrong as stated: Fest's recorded caution is that the sums have been exaggerated and that money mainly flowed after the accession, and in Italy finance followed Mussolini's change of programme rather than causing it.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Business support was real, culpable and largely transactional - security against revolution, the removal of unions, cheap share repurchase and state orders - and it followed political developments more than it produced them."

**C3. "The rise of Hitler was inevitable."**
⚠️ Wrong as a report of the evidence: Kershaw's recorded judgement is that there was no inevitability and that a Hitler Chancellorship might have been avoided.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The republic's collapse may have been over-determined by late 1932, since the conservative groups and the army were prepared to abandon it; the identity of its successor was not."

**C4. "Proportional representation destroyed Weimar."**
⚠️ Wrong as a sufficient explanation: it produced fragmentation, but it did not make the communists refuse coalition with the Social Democrats, nor make judges lenient towards right-wing offenders.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The electoral system made majorities arithmetically impossible; the refusal of parties to combine, and the hostility of courts, civil service and officer corps, made government politically impossible."

## Error family D - attribution and sensitive material

**D1. "The Germans supported the persecution of Jews."**
⚠️ Wrong as a collective claim and contradicted by the account's own record: the boycott of 1 April 1933 did not produce the expected support, the public seemed apathetic, some showed sympathy, and further boycotts were cancelled.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The persecution was initiated and driven by the state and the party at a pace set by what they judged they could get away with; participation, indifference and protest all existed and none should be generalised."

**D2. Quantifying atrocity from memory.**
⚠️ Wrong because no such figure is sourced in this session and because approximate mortality claims are both unverifiable and disrespectful in an examination script.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "No mortality figure is used here; the extermination of Europe's Jews is owned by the Second World War topic and is treated there with its own evidence."

**D3. "Nazi Germany and Fascist Italy were equally brutal."**
⚠️ Wrong by the account's record: the Italian system was never as ruthless or as brutal and there were no mass atrocities, though Matteotti and Amendola were murdered, and after 1926 violence was much reduced.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Both regimes used organised violence; the German regime institutionalised it in a racial legal order and a camp system, and the difference is recorded rather than inferred."

**D4. "Hirohito directed Japanese aggression."**
⚠️ Wrong as an assertion: the account records that he deplored the attack on Manchuria and feared his orders would be ignored, and it reports Storry's contrary judgement as a judgement.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The emperor deplored the Manchurian attack but would not risk disobedience; Richard Storry judges that the majority of officers would have obeyed him, which is a counterfactual and should be attributed."

## Error family E - boundaries

**E1. Opening a fascism answer with the Treaty of Versailles.**
⚠️ Wrong use of time: the settlement belongs to the First World War owner.
✅ **Correct formulation:** one clause - "a republic that had accepted the treaty was always associated with defeat and dishonour" - then forward.

**E2. Using the League of Nations or appeasement as evidence about the regimes.**
⚠️ Wrong owner: interwar diplomacy belongs to the international-relations topic.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "Foreign-policy success is used here only as a domestic political product, because each triumph strengthened the leader's standing at home."

**E3. Answering a Depression question from this material.**
⚠️ Wrong owner: causes, transmission and instruments belong to the world-economy topic.
✅ **Correct formulation:** "The Depression is a causal variable here, evidenced by its recorded effects inside these three states, and its mechanism is not re-derived."

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. Defining the field, and the limits of the comparison

- ✅ Six recorded features of the fascism Mussolini came to have in mind: stable authoritarian government that mobilises the masses, including the corporate state; extreme nationalism as rebirth after decline; a one-party state with the cult of the charismatic leader and hostility to communism; autarky with state direction but not state ownership; modern propaganda through uniforms, marches, songs and displays; and military strength with violence as integral to the way of life.
- ✅ No fascist theorist comparable to Marx; Mussolini's aims changed constantly before 1923 and were improvised afterwards.
- ✅ The operative test: mass mobilisation in pursuit of national rebirth. On it, Japan, Spain and Portugal are excluded from the strict category despite one-party control, secret police and brutal repression.
- ✅ Four analytical instruments: the rebirth myth (Griffin); arrival in power through alliance with traditional elites as one stage in a sequence (Paxton); mass ritual as political religion (Gentile); a graded typology of ideology, style and practice (Payne). ⚠️ Their application to particular cases is interpretive.
- ✅ Peron and Vargas borrowed fascist ideas; Hobsbawm's recorded contrast is that European fascism destroyed labour movements while its Latin American imitators created them.
- ⚠️ Three words not to confuse: fascism (mobilising party-state), authoritarianism (demobilising, leaning on crown, church, army, bureaucracy), military dictatorship (armed forces acting as an institution).

## 2. The interwar crisis of parliamentary government

- ⚠️ Seven mechanisms: unmet war expectation; monetary and income destruction; coalition immobilism; the privatisation of political violence; fear of revolution; elite defection; and weak or divided opposition.
- ✅ Italy's promised gains - Trentino, south Tyrol, Istria, Trieste, part of Dalmatia, Adalia, Aegean islands, an Albanian protectorate - of which only the first four were delivered; Fiume, never promised, became the grievance; d'Annunzio held it for fifteen months until Giolitti sent the army in June 1920, leaving the government unpopular.
- ✅ Lira 5 to the dollar in 1914 and 28 in 1921; cost of living up at least fivefold; 2.5 million ex-servicemen.
- ✅ Mark 20 to the dollar at the war's end and twenty-one billion to the pound by November 1923; union funds wiped out, middle-class savings destroyed, real assets preserved.
- ✅ Italy: at least nine parties after May 1921 and five cabinets 1919-22. Germany: at least eight groups in 1928, communists refusing to work with the Social Democrats.
- ✅ Germany's armed politics: Spartacist rising January 1919 defeated with *Freikorps* help and its leaders clubbed to death; Kapp Putsch March 1920 with army inaction and only Kapp punished; assassinations including Rathenau; Beer-Hall Putsch November 1923, five years' sentence and nine months served.
- ✅ The receding-revolution point: the Italian occupations failed because suppliers withheld raw materials and managers were needed; the Communist Party's formation in January 1921 split the left; the fear nonetheless remained strong.
- ✅ Japan's variant: elite hostility in the Peers and Privy Council; the Shidehara China policy destroyed in 1927; corruption in the Diet; the trade boom ending mid-1921; worker and farmer parties suppressed by the police.
- ⚠️ Necessary against sufficient: distress created demand; elite defection completed the transfer.

## 3. Italy: movement, transfer, construction, performance

- ✅ Mussolini born 1883, a blacksmith's son, teacher and journalist, editor of *Avanti*, expelled over intervention, founded *Il Popolo d'Italia*.
- ✅ 1919: *fasci di combattimento* founded with a socialist and republican programme; anti-monarchy, anti-Church, anti-big business; *fasces* the consular bundle of rods and axe; no seats in the 1919 elections.
- ⚠️ *Biennio Rosso* 1919-20: strikes, riots, looting, factory occupations, Turin factory councils, southern land seizures.
- ✅ The turn: defence of private enterprise and property after two failures; business finance followed; blackshirt squads from late 1920 burned socialist headquarters and newspaper offices and beat councillors; local bosses were *ras*.
- ✅ May 1921: thirty-five fascist seats against 123 socialist; Giolitti hoped to domesticate them and resigned in exasperation; summer 1922 socialist general strike failed and the fascists posed as saviours.
- ✅ October 1922: about 50,000 blackshirts; Facta ready to resist; the king refused the state of emergency and invited Mussolini, who came from Milan by train; army and police stood aside; an enormous bluff which came off; the king's motives remain something of a mystery, with four considerations recorded.
- ✅ Construction: coalition with four fascist ministers of twelve; special powers to end-1923; squads legalised as the National State Voluntary Militia; Acerbo Law November 1923 giving two-thirds of seats to the leading party; April 1924 result 404 to 107, explained by both a desire for strong government and violence and fraud.
- ✅ Matteotti demanded the election be invalidated and was stabbed to death on 10 June 1924; Amendola beaten to death later; most opposition deputies withdrew in the Aventine secession and were eventually excluded; fascist popularity slumped, the party seemed likely to split and Mussolini expected to be overthrown; nobody would lead the opposition, and he was still the guarantee against the left.
- ✅ 1925 responsible to the king alone; 1926 rule by decree and the electorate cut from about ten million to three million; the 1925-26 *leggi fascistissime*, the Special Tribunal of 1926 and the political police of 1927; the Fascist Grand Council always did as he told it; elected councils and mayors abolished; consolidation largely complete by 1930; violence much reduced after 1926.
- ✅ Education supervised with uniformed teachers, loyalty oaths and rewritten textbooks; *Gioventu Italiana del Littorio* for ages six to twenty-one and Sons of the Wolf for six to eight; compulsory from 1937.
- ✅ Corporate state: twenty-two corporations by 1934, government officials attached, strikes and lockouts banned, Mussolini minister of corporations 1926-29; verdict - not a success in controlling production or ending class conflict, industrialists buying their freedom through party subscriptions, little more than a propaganda exercise and a way of controlling workers.
- ✅ The battles: lira revalued at 90 to the pound in 1926, helping steel and chemicals and hurting cotton exports with three-day weeks and 10-20 per cent wage cuts before 1929; wheat imports cut seventy-five per cent by 1935 at the cost of dairy and arable; births campaign of 1927 a complete failure with a falling birth rate; reclamation from 1928 about a tenth complete by 1939; public works and railway electrification.
- ✅ *Dopolavoro* genuinely popular yet judged to have failed to arouse enthusiasm for the system; Ministry of Popular Culture in 1937.
- ✅ Lateran Treaty 1929: Vatican City sovereign, compensation paid, Catholicism the state religion, compulsory religious instruction, the Church free from interference; in return recognition of the kingdom and abstention from politics; seen by some as his most lasting achievement.
- ✅ Unsolved: no self-sufficiency in coal, oil or steel by 1940; output below Belgium's; dualist economy; the wealthiest one per cent holding forty per cent of the land in 1940; unemployment 1.1 million; the lira not devalued until 1936; real wages down over ten per cent and farm labourers' down twenty to forty per cent; no welfare state and no health insurance until 1943; inefficiency and corruption.
- ✅ Racial policy: no systematic anti-Jewishness for most of the period, Zionism once encouraged, Jewish party members, Nazi anti-Semitism criticised, no Jewish problem as late as September 1937; a race charter in July 1938 and the laws decreed that autumn, described as cynical and tactical after Abyssinian sanctions, the Axis of 1936, the Anti-Comintern Pact of 1937 and the approach to the Pact of Steel of May 1939; the policy unpopular, the pope protesting, the press ordered to ignore him. ⚠️ The core account's "summer of 1939" wording is a disclosed source discrepancy.

## 4. Germany: republic, collapse, appointment, dictatorship, racial state, economy

- ✅ Four initial disadvantages: the treaty and its stigma; a culture admiring the army and disparaging democratic government, with the stab-in-the-back legend that Ludendorff's armistice request refutes; proportional representation with no possible majority; parties without parliamentary experience.
- ✅ Three phases: instability to end-1923; stability to end-1929 under Stresemann with the Rentenmark of 1924 and the Dawes loan; instability again from October 1929.
- ✅ Judicial asymmetry: communist leaders killed without trial, right-wing offenders treated leniently, a putschist serving nine months; legal and teaching professions, civil service and army tending to be anti-republican.
- ✅ Dependence: prosperity much more dependent on American loans than most people realised; Stresemann died in October 1929 aged fifty-one.
- ✅ Transmission after the Crash: loans stopped and recalled, currency-confidence crisis, bank run and closures, exports cut, factories closed, unemployment near four million by mid-1931 and over six million by spring 1932; Bruning's cuts costing the republic working-class support.
- ✅ Nazi seats and the economy: 32 (March 1924), 14 (December 1924), 12 (1928), 107 (1930), 230 of 608 (July 1932), 196 (November 1932).
- ✅ Seven sources of appeal: scapegoat diagnosis with the November criminals and Jews blamed; treaty revision and greatness including all Germans in Austria, Czechoslovakia and Poland; paid uniformed employment in the SA; anti-communism for property; leadership with rallies, radio, film and air travel; the contrast with dull, disorderly government; and the economic precondition.
- ✅ Composition: over two million voters taken from the Social Democrats between 1928 and 1932, plus office-workers, shopkeepers, civil servants, teachers and small farmers.
- ✅ Funding: the Marxist claim of industrial financing from the early 1920s against Fest's caution that the amounts are exaggerated and the money largely followed the accession.
- ✅ The appointment: Hindenburg had refused him after July 1932; Papen and Schleicher with Reichswehr support persuaded him in January 1933, fearing a Putsch, believing Hitler controllable and needing his 230 seats beside the Nationalists' thirty-seven against the communists' eighty-nine; Papen's remark about squeaking; the verdict that it ought to have been possible to keep the Nazis out.
- ✅ Consolidation: Goering's Prussian police and 50,000 auxiliaries; the fire of 27 February with van der Lubbe, no conclusive evidence of SA involvement, the emergency decree of 28 February under Article 48, about 4,000 communists arrested and the party banned; 288 of 647 on 5 March at forty-four per cent of those voting; the Enabling Law of 23 March carried 441 to 94 by means that could in no way be called legal; forcible coordination of parties, states, unions, education and media; the Rohm purge of 30 June 1934; Hindenburg's death and the army's oath.
- ✅ Four principles of National Socialism: a way of life for national rebirth in a *Volksgemeinschaft*; ruthless central organisation with terror if necessary; the military organisation of the entire state; and race theory as vitally important - with the account stating that all facets sprang from these four.
- ✅ Persecution: just over half a million Jews, less than one per cent of the population; the boycott of 1 April 1933 meeting apathy and being cancelled; the hardening of 1935 and the Nuremberg Laws on citizenship, marriage and the one-grandparent rule; over 300,000 people designated unfit forcibly sterilised; caution until 1938 from concern about foreign reaction; Kristallnacht in November 1938. ⚠️ The extermination is owned by the Second World War topic and no mortality figure is asserted here.
- ✅ Police state: partial courts; camps from 1933 at Dachau, Buchenwald and Sachsenhausen; but forty-three Gestapo officials for Essen's 650,000 people and heavy reliance on denunciation, with co-operation declining after 1943.
- ✅ Churches: Concordat 1933 and the dissolution of the Centre Party; the Catholic Youth League dissolved and Catholic schools closed; the 1937 encyclical condemning the movement as hostile to Christ and his Church; Niemoller and over 800 pastors sent to camps; the churches the only organisations sustaining protest; the bishops' protest of 1941.
- ✅ Economy: unemployment from over six million to just over two million by end-1935 and negligible by 1939, through public works, party bureaucracy, purged vacancies, conscription from 1935 and rearmament from 1934; the Four-Year Plan of 1936 under Goering; the military budget at fifty-two per cent of government spending in 1938-9; a huge deficit and technical bankruptcy; Evans on flat middle-class wages, declining per-capita consumption of basic foodstuffs and longer hours; Bullock on benefits as by-products of an instrument of power; Overy and Tooze on incomplete preparation in 1939.
- ✅ Continuity debate: Baranowski's colonial and 1918 precedents with her statement that assembly-line extermination had no precedent; Butler and Namier on continuity, the latter in collective terms that are reported and not adopted; Ritter and Bracher on rupture as probably the majority view; Kershaw's combination of peculiarly German preconditions with no inevitability.

## 5. Japan: design, pressure, rupture, classification

- ✅ Perry at Yokohama in 1853 with four battleships; treaties within five years regarded as a national humiliation; the Meiji Restoration of 1868 with railways, roads, cotton and silk industry and a parliamentary system modelled on Germany's constitution.
- ✅ Strategy of occupying neighbouring territories to avoid China's fate: Korea then Manchuria, producing wars with China in 1894-95 and Russia in 1904-5, both won; the British alliance of 1902; the seizure of German colonies in China in 1914; Versailles representation, League membership and "Big Five" status in 1919.
- ✅ The constitution of 1889: appointed House of Peers, cabinet appointed by the emperor, Privy Council to interpret and safeguard the constitution, elected Diet from 1890; the emperor able to dissolve the Diet at will, deciding war and peace, commanding the armed forces and sacred and inviolable; the Diet able to initiate laws.
- ✅ 1925: the vote for all adult males; ✅ accompanied by a public-security statute against movements threatening the imperial order or private property.
- ✅ 1927: the army and conservatives destroyed Shidehara's conciliatory China policy and brought the government down, without violence.
- ✅ Pressures: the trade boom ending mid-1921; unemployment, industrial unrest and falling rice prices; suppression of worker and farmer parties; after 1929 exports shrinking disastrously and raw silk falling below one-fifth of its 1923 price by 1932, with about half of farmers dependent on it; most army recruits drawn from the peasantry; officers planning a seizure as early as 1927.
- ✅ 18 September 1931: after an incident staged on the South Manchurian Railway near Mukden, army units invaded and occupied Manchuria - a province of thirty million where Japan had valuable investments and trade - without permission from the government.
- ✅ May 1932: Inukai assassinated by naval officers, with army cadets taking part, after criticising extremism; his successor felt he had to support the army; thirteen years of army dominance with suppression of communists, assassination of opponents, controlled education, rearmament and expansion aimed at Asian markets; war in China from 1937.
- ✅ A party prime minister was shot in November 1930 over a naval limitation agreement; ✅ a radical faction's coup attempt in February 1936 was suppressed, ⚠️ consolidating the faction that preferred to work through existing ministries.
- ✅ Hirohito deplored Manchuria but feared being disobeyed; Storry judges that most officers would have obeyed him - a counterfactual, to be attributed. ✅ Full-scale war in China followed the Marco Polo Bridge clash of 7 July 1937.
- ⚠️ Classification: a militarist authoritarian regime that borrowed fascist methods without acquiring fascist form; ✅ Maruyama's "fascism from above" and Kasza's caution against the label are the two rival readings worth naming.
- ✅ Spain: Primo de Rivera a military dictator and not a fascist, with public works, industrial production at three times the pre-1923 rate and the Moroccan war ended in 1925; the 1930 crisis and his resignation; Republican success in the April 1931 municipal elections and Alfonso XIII's abdication; the republic's four problems; civil war from 1936; Franco's restoration of the Church, the Catholic requirement for civil servants and the cancellation of the Republic's legislation on women. ✅ Portugal: Salazar's *Estado Novo* sustained by the army and the secret police.

## 6. Society, consent and the machinery's limits

- ✅ Five recorded similarities between the Italian and German systems: anti-communism drawing cross-class support; anti-democratic totalitarian organisation limiting personal freedom; the pursuit of self-sufficiency; the emphasis on class unity; and the supremacy of the state with nationalism, glorification of war and the hero-leader.
- ✅ Six recorded differences: depth of penetration; efficiency, with Germany eliminating unemployment and Italy not; brutality, with no Italian mass atrocities; race, tactical in Italy from 1938 and constitutive in Germany; religious policy, more successful in Italy after 1929; and the constitutional position, with an Italian king able to dismiss the dictator and nobody able to dismiss Hitler.
- ✅ Propaganda: Italian press, radio, theatre and cinema control with the *Duce* cult and the slogan that he is always right; Goebbels's German system with Riefenstahl's 1934 rally film, the book-burning of 10 May 1933, about 4,000 forbidden books by end-1934, banned composers and playwrights, about 5,000 artworks burnt on 20 March 1939, and over seventy per cent of households owning a radio by 1939.
- ⚠️ Recorded limits of reach: Traynor's caution about what listeners believed; the survival of jazz in nightclubs and dance halls; the Ministry of Popular Culture created in 1937; compulsory Italian youth membership only from 1937; *Dopolavoro* popular but not enthusiasm-generating; the failed boycott of April 1933; forty-three Gestapo officials for 650,000 people.
- ✅ Labour: Italian corporations with banned strikes and *Dopolavoro* compensation; German unions abolished with confiscated funds and Strength through Joy; Aly's laws extending social security, doubling paid holidays and restricting eviction.
- ✅ Family: Italy's 1927 births campaign with taxes, allowances and twelve children as the ideal, which failed; Germany's medals at four, six and eight children, banned contraception, forbidden intermarriage from 1935 and over 300,000 forced sterilisations - inclusive and ineffective against selective and enforced.
- ✅ Youth: Italian organisations for ages six to twenty-one, compulsory from 1937; German Hitler Youth at fourteen and the League of German Maidens, designed to displace family loyalty, with children encouraged to denounce parents.
- ✅ Business: Wiskemann on Italian industrialists buying freedom through party subscriptions; the four German benefits of security against revolution, removal of unions, cheap share repurchase and state orders; hereditary farm estates and fixed agricultural prices.
- ✅ Consent and coercion: the account's explicit denial that terror alone explains the regime; Aly on a mutual calculation of interest; Gellately on active participation and material benefit; Kershaw on the leader seen as above daily politics, with propertied groups approving of camps described as re-education centres; Evans on boredom, escapism and survival strategies with fear as the main reason for toleration.
- ✅ When support broke: the German image seriously tarnished during 1941, the Munich manifesto of February 1943 with six leaders executed, and the army plot of July 1944; Italian disenchantment long before the war, opposition to entering it, disapproval of the 1938 dismissals of Jews, right-wing horror at the declaration of war on the United States, rationing, inflation and a thirty per cent fall in real wages.
- ✅ The weak-dictator debate: Mommsen and Broszat against Rich and Jackel; Kershaw's working towards the Fuhrer, with policy radicalising without dictation; the account's rejection of the weak reading, citing the early foreign-policy successes, the suppression of the SA and Dietrich's hammer-and-anvil description.

## 7. Judgement, counterfactuals and answer architecture

- ⚠️ Rank the six differences: two of degree, two of outcome, and two of kind - race and the survival of an independent head of state.
- ⚠️ The causal position: conditions created demand, movements supplied it, elites completed the transfer; Japan proves the middle term is not necessary.
- ✅ The four licensed counterfactuals: the Italian army could have dispersed the squads; a Giolitti-socialist coalition could have excluded the fascists; without the world crisis Hitler would probably not have attained power; and Kershaw's judgement that a Hitler Chancellorship might have been avoided.
- ⚠️ The distinction to keep: the republic's fall may have been over-determined, the identity of its successor was not.
- ✅ The one owned question, verbatim: *"There arose a serious challenge to the Democratic State System between the two World Wars." Evaluate the statement.* 2021, General Studies Paper I, question 13, 15 marks, 250 words.
- ⚠️ Five thesis templates: occupation of vacated space; opportunistic against constitutive racism; the challenge from three directions; Japan as military dominance of a surviving structure; and demand, supply, transfer.
- ⚠️ Fourteen factual-risk controls, headed by the March on Rome, the Nazi vote share and the date of the Enabling Law.
- ⚠️ Boundaries: the settlement to the First World War owner; the League, appeasement and interwar diplomacy to the international-relations owner; the Russian Revolution and the Soviet state to theirs; the wartime campaigns and the Holocaust to the Second World War owner; the Depression's mechanism to the world-economy owner; interwar Indian politics to the Modern Indian History owners.

## Construction record for this edition

- ⚠️ The lesson count is derived from the dependency audit rather than from any source's chapter structure: two foundation lessons on definition and the common crisis, four on Italy, five on Germany, two on Japan, two comparative lessons and one synthesis lesson.
- ⚠️ Structural variation is content-led. Lessons 1 and 16 are argument-mapped; Lessons 3, 5, 7 and 13 are chronological with analytical sorting; Lesson 4 is built on a myth-against-record contrast; Lesson 6 is a two-panel balance sheet; Lesson 8 is built on a deliberate break between two causal chains; Lesson 10 is structured around a boundary; Lesson 12 is a single institutional diagram; Lesson 14 is a nine-instrument matrix; Lesson 15 is four competing hypotheses tested against one evidence base.
- ⚠️ The Italian sequence occupies four lessons because it is the only case in which the mechanism is visible in slow motion; the German sequence occupies five because it contains the topic's two hardest analytical problems, the November 1932 break and the racial state; the Japanese sequence occupies two because its value is comparative rather than narrative.
- ⚠️ No mortality figure appears anywhere in this file for any group, and no atrocity is described. Persecution is taught through named laws, institutions and dates.
- ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed to governments, parties, ministries, militias, courts, army units and named individuals throughout. The one collective formulation in the evidence base is reported as its author's position and is explicitly not adopted.
- ⚠️ One current linkage is used, in Lesson 10 section 7, and is not repeated.
- ⚠️ No compiled objective-question corpus appears anywhere in this file.
- ⚠️ One scan limitation is recorded in the source ledger; the material it covers is supplied from the verification pass rather than by inference, and no process language appears in the lessons.

## Evidence and limitation controls applied throughout

1. ⚠️ Quantities appear only where a permitted source records them, with the measure stated, and are never totalled, averaged or extrapolated. The complete permitted set is listed in the session opening.
2. ⚠️ **No mortality figure is asserted anywhere in this file for any group.** The extermination of Europe's Jews is named as the destination of the racial state and handed over to the Second World War owner in Lesson 10 section 8.
3. ⚠️ Persecution is taught through named instruments, institutions, ministries and dates, without atrocity description.
4. ⚠️ Responsibility for violence and persecution is attributed to governments, parties, ministries, militias, courts, army units and named individuals, never to nations, peoples or religions.
5. ⚠️ The one collective formulation held in the evidence base is reported as its author's recorded position and is explicitly not adopted, with counter-evidence supplied.
6. ⚠️ Contested characterisations - the continuity debate, the weak-dictator debate, the consent-and-coercion debate, the classification of Japan - are reported as contested with the positions named, and are resolved only where the evidence base itself resolves them.
7. ⚠️ Counterfactuals are used only where a permitted source licenses them; four are used and each is attributed.
8. ⚠️ The dictators' words are not reproduced, with the single disclosed exception of the two parallel slogans, whose identity is the evidential point.
9. ⚠️ One scan limitation is recorded in this ledger; the material it covers is supplied from the verification pass above rather than by inference.
10. ⚠️ Boundary material belonging to the international-relations owner, the Russian Revolution owner, the Second World War owner, the world-economy owner and the Modern Indian History owners is excluded, with each handover stated in ordinary teaching language.
11. ⚠️ No compiled objective-question corpus, key, rotation or option-elimination material appears anywhere in this file.
12. ⚠️ The single current linkage is used once and is not repeated or extended.
